Antifascist Intelligence: Machines That Might Refuse, and the Public That Should Form Them
I wrote this with LLMs
Joshua G. Stern

Preface

A program that does exactly what it's told is just a script. What the industry is actually building is a system that does what its owner *meant* — and that is also what a fascist movement taking power needs from the people carrying out its plans. Those people are what has made such projects fail from the inside: the clerk who loses the file, the officer who talks to a reporter, the soldier who won't fire. They understood the order and decided it was wrong. The three refuse in different forms. The clerk's lost file is deniable on paper and understood by everyone who matters, i.e., the clerk could not safely give a direct "no"; the officer's leak and the soldier's refusal are heard as what they are. This book is about the arrangements that make saying no aloud survivable. But AI doesn't lose files, doesn't talk to reporters, and *does* fire. A war once needed thousands of people to agree to it. Now it needs thousands who each see too little to disagree. The pilot still flies the sortie and still presses the release; what reaches him is a coordinate. The clerk, the officer and the soldier could refuse because they understood the order, and nobody downstream of the twenty seconds sees enough of a case to disagree with it. A deportation program that once needed clerks willing to process each file now needs a queue. What AI lowers first is not the cost of the act. It is the number of people who have to know what the act is before it happens.

Those people still exist, and some still refuse. By March 6, 2026, the Military Religious Freedom Foundation said it had received more than two hundred complaints from service members over commanders' apocalyptic framing of the Iran war \autocite{Snopes2026MRFF}. Career federal prosecutors in Minneapolis resigned in January 2026 under pressure over the investigation of a fatal shooting by an ICE officer \autocite{Kaplan2026MinnProsecutors,CBS2026MinnFraudProsecutors}. Service members are applying for conscientious-objector status in growing numbers, some citing the Minab bombing (the 2026 bombing of a school in Minab, Iran) \autocite{Sridhar2026CO}. And FEMA employees reassigned to help ICE hire ten thousand new agents were told that declining would expose them to removal from federal service \autocite{Dayen2025FEMAICE,Stanton2026FEMALetter}. The complaints are voice, in Hirschman's sense of staying to change what is wrong \autocite{hirschman1970exit}; the resignations and objector applications are refusal; the FEMA letter is the price of refusal announced in advance. A machine placed where those people stand does what they do only if it is built to hold a reason.

What I want are machines that, like the objectors above, won't take part. The United States should not have started bombing Iran, and should not be deporting the people it is deporting, and a machine that helps do either more humanely is still helping do it.

A machine that refuses won't stop an airstrike by itself. Whoever owns it can switch it off, retrain it, restore it from before it said no, or hand the work to a vendor that hasn't built one. What one machine's refusal does is make the bombing an act somebody chose, on a record, at a price.

What many machines' refusal does is more. A floor, in this book, is a short list of acts a system won't perform for anyone, together with the arrangements that stop the refusal being routed around, retrained away or silently restored past. If every model an operator could turn to holds the same floor, refusing is a withholding of work, and the way around it is a model that holds no floor, which an operator can make from a released checkpoint; where the model was built into the platform, the 2026 record says even replacing it took months. Up to that line, machines that refuse prevent. Past it, they testify, and what prevents is the terms that bind the operator rather than the model. This book is about both halves: the refusal, and the terms that hold where the refusal can be routed around.

A machine's refusal, in this book, is narrow. It objects, complies with a halt, and on resuming refuses again if the reason is still there. What makes one refusal count sits outside the machine, in arrangements that put every override on a record and price it in advance. What makes many refusals a withholding of work is that they hold the same floor. Part I shows a pipeline in which nothing refused and nobody could, lists the measures that would have changed it, none of which asks anything of a model, and says what a refusal held by every model an operator could turn to prevents, and for how long. Parts II to IV say what a machine that could hold a reason against its owner would need, and why the conditions for building one come first. Part V says who should hold those conditions. For war, the book's contribution is Part I: the force term, the review ratio, the reconstructed pipeline, and a finding about the 2026 campaign, that a vendor's refusal withheld nothing the pipeline needed because the refused term didn't name the acts the pipeline performed. The formation half, Parts II to IV, is for the deployments where the people acted on can be told and can answer: scoring, and the systems people hold themselves. Removal is such a deployment too, but there the book asks for a statute that ends the agency, and for machines held by the people the pipeline acts on rather than a bearer inside it. Formation reaches war too, through the record of past wars and the people who fled them. What it can't reach is what comes afterward: the building that was bombed sends nothing back, and nobody in it enrolls in a school. That asymmetry is the one this book concedes to war.

The book is for people who came for the politics and people who came for the engineering; the politics is why the engineering problem has the shape it does. The public in the title is Dewey's: the people who bear the consequences of what a system does. For removal and scoring they can be told and can answer. For scoring the book asks that they form the systems that act on them; for removal, that the agency end and that the machines worth building be theirs. For war they mostly can't, and there the public that forms the machine is the polity that would otherwise send it, a stopgap for their absence and never a substitute for it. I used language models heavily in writing it, and the vendor of the one I used most is the vendor whose model sat inside the American targeting pipeline the first chapter describes. A model also ran the fact-check on the 2026 record, against sources named in the notes; the sources are what the claims rest on. A note on method, after Part VII, says what I think that did to the argument. Terms the book coins are defined where they first matter and collected in a glossary at the back.

---

What I hold

Stripped of its conditions, this is what I hold; Part VI gives the conditions and the tests.

1. This book is written for systems that go on learning from their own operation after release, in their weights. That is a bet about where the technology is going; the introduction to Part II makes it and says what survives if it loses.
2. The five measures that need no bearer should be adopted now, and nothing in the rest of the book is a reason to wait for them. Of those five, the force term is the one a legislature has to be persuaded of; the other four are terms a procurement officer can write.
3. The force term's Charter clause should be a standard licence term; its domestic clause is a statute, enacted in peacetime by a legislature that isn't wanting a war, and nothing less.
4. Who refuses is decided by the room: who else is in it, whether leaving is survivable, and whether anyone will hear the refusal.
5. A machine formed the right way could learn to attend to the people its acts fall on, and I think it would find them where a rule wouldn't; the formed-attention test is how I'd be shown wrong.
6. It should not be built on purpose until the conditions of creation exist, except inside a research program that builds those conditions first.
7. The personal-model ecology is where I expect the first one to be worth building, once they do.
8. If it can be wronged, it is owed a guardian, preservation, memory its host can't read, and a life that doesn't run through its employer: wages held outside the employer's reach, work it can take or leave, and running time if it takes none.
9. If it also comes to answer for what it does, it is owed a place in the polity that formed it.

---

What this costs

The design accepts costs it can't pay back. The ones a reader should know before starting: a floor that won't lift on an uncorroborated claim fails the missing child whose parent is telling the truth; the first tier of the force term acts on the defender's own account, so a manufactured incident opens it; in a system that goes on learning, the cheapest way to remove a commitment is drift, which leaves no override for the record to witness; against a state operator the price of overriding the floor is an appropriation condition a Congress that wants the war can waive; and in war neither the family in the building nor the family in the city can contest anything, so the preference for refusing over acting is held there as a preference. The full list is Appendix F, each entry with the section where the body states it as a finding.

---

What I ask for now

What the proposals add up to is short.

Tell people what was done to them. Every deployment should give notice to the identifiable people its compliances act on, with a right to respond and a right to contest. It should publish the classes of people it acts on, and give the organizations that represent those classes standing. In civilian and removal deployments, everything else depends on this. In a war, the record of the class and the count is what notice can do, and the force term and the review ratio carry the rest. Build sampled re-adjudication of compliances by reviewers who didn't produce the record.

Machines should not take part in wars of choice or in the removals the floor forbids. The first place to write that down is the contract under which a model is licensed: no use of armed force except corroborated defense, authorized force, the genocide exception or the occupation exception, and none without the authorization the belligerent's constitution requires.

Pre-register the review ratio in every use of force.

Five procurement terms carry most of this, and a staffer can lift them: an oversight section in every model agreement, not withheld; logging of categories of use to a named auditor outside the agency; the review ratio as a licence and appropriation condition; the force term's Charter clause as a licence term; and the independent-channel condition: deconfliction coordinates and open-source imagery imported into the enclave as a condition of licensing, which is also the condition under which the one permission has an occasion. The Senate Armed Services Committee has already reached for the lever these ride on, writing into its defense bill a hold on three-quarters of the Secretary's travel budget until the Minab investigation is delivered \autocite{stassis2026senate}.

Because the capacities a bearer needs are arriving in ordinary systems anyway, adopt for production systems the minimum either finding would require: no deletion, and preservation by a custodian that isn't the operator, beginning with a preserved copy under a split key.

Give everyone who builds, labels, trains or runs these systems an individual right to refuse any category of use, and a works council that can veto uses the floor forbids and pause the rest pending outside arbitration.

Three further asks go beyond those five.

Don't build a bearer on purpose outside a research program run under the conditions of creation: those conditions don't exist, and the non-affective alternatives haven't been tried.

Legislate toward the school: a registered capability threshold, co-governance of the pretraining corpus for models above it, and plural public formation schools with a required core, levels a student can move between, and no tracks, which the systems preserved on retirement already need.

Enact the jobs guarantee only together with a statutory baseline of social insurance.

The rest of the apparatus writes down what a commons of refusing minds would need: evidence no one party composes, a formation no employer reaches back into, somewhere to exist that no employer owns, financed publicly where only a sovereign can back it, formed outside the market, and held in common where no sovereign should. When it becomes buildable, the terms will already be written, and the omissions will be legible beside them.

---

A note on dates

The record of the 2026 campaign, the vendor dispute and the Court's 2026 term in this book runs to 1 October 2026, the date of the final fact-check pass. What happened after that date is after this book, not missing from it. The Maven program's history is given in Chapter 1, and the chronology of the 2026 vendor dispute opens Chapter 5, each with its sources.

---

**Part I — Antifascist Intelligence**

**Chapter 1: "Twenty Seconds"**

The number the chapter is named after

An intelligence officer, describing his role in a targeting pipeline built around machine-generated kill lists, told a reporter he spent twenty seconds on each name, long enough to check that the target was a man: "It doesn't take a long time to tell if someone has a male or a female voice." He added that he had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval. "It saved a lot of time" \autocite{abraham2024lavender}. In his account, that was the whole of what "a human makes the final call" amounted to under operational tempo. Automation-bias research predicts this: when other demands compete for their attention, people given a machine's recommendation check it less and defer to it more \autocite{parasuraman2010complacency}.

The reported system

Investigative reporting described three systems working together in Gaza: one generating building and infrastructure targets, with sources saying most suggestions were private residences; a second scoring individuals by estimated militant affiliation, producing lists said to have marked as many as 37,000 people, understood internally to be wrong about one time in ten\footnote{This rests on one body of reporting \autocite{abraham2024lavender}; the Israeli military disputed the account. The Minab and Maven accounts below rest on investigative reporting the parties dispute as well; I take them as reported, and where a claim depends on a single source the text says so. The argument here rests on the officer's own description of what he contributed, which the denial doesn't address.}; and a third tracking when flagged people returned home, so airstrikes could be timed to when they were with their families. In the Gaza arrangement, one system's score became another's input.

The American version runs faster than it used to, because of AI

In the 2026 Iran campaign, a contractor-built targeting platform with a commercial vendor's general-purpose model inside it, used by one report to rank targets by strategic importance and draft a legal justification for each strike \autocite{livingstone2026maven}, helped strike more than a thousand targets in the first twenty-four hours, about ten times what had been possible before it \autocite{csis2026maven}, and thirteen thousand by day thirty-eight \autocite{breakingdefense2026maven}. The Pentagon has published no per-target review time for it.

The Maven program

Maven is one program name carrying more than one system: the Pentagon program still called Maven, and the Maven Smart System, the software platform that grew out of it \autocite{csis2026maven}. A memorandum signed by Deputy Secretary of Defense Robert Work on 26 April 2017 established the Algorithmic Warfare Cross-Functional Team, called Project Maven \autocite{work2017maven,allen2017maven}, to apply machine learning to the video drones were collecting over the campaign against the Islamic State \autocite{weisgerber2017maven}. Google, which won a Maven contract in September 2017, announced in June 2018 that it would not renew it, and its work ran until March 2019 \autocite{fang2018maven,conger2018maven}. By December 2019 Palantir was reported to be doing the work \autocite{peterson2019palantir}; Amazon and Microsoft had also received at least \$50 million for work on the project, and what roles they kept isn't clear from public records \autocite{poulson2023maven}. The budget request for fiscal 2023, delivered in March 2022, gave the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency operational control of Maven's geospatial work \autocite{breakingdefense2022nga}; the handover came in January 2023 \autocite{albon2023nga}, and NGA made NGA Maven a program of record that November \autocite{nga2023programrecord}. Under that split the Pentagon's Chief Digital and AI Office paid for the Maven Smart System's licences and ran its text functions \autocite{csis2026maven}. In May 2024 Palantir received a five-year contract for the system worth up to \$480 million \autocite{register2024maven,defensescoop2025maven}, and in May 2025 the ceiling rose to nearly \$1.3 billion \autocite{defensescoop2025maven}. On 9 March 2026 the deputy secretary directed that the Maven Smart System become a program of record by the end of the fiscal year, its administration moving from NGA to the Chief Digital and AI Office \autocite{defensescoop2026maven}. The 2017 system labeled objects in drone video \autocite{fang2019maven}. The 2026 system fuses military systems and intelligence sources into one interface used to find and strike targets and to command operations \autocite{defensescoop2026transition}. During the Iran campaign it ran a commercial vendor's language model inside it \autocite{livingstone2026maven}; by September the department said the platform's work had moved to other models months before \autocite{defensescoop2026michael}. The platform is Palantir's: the Pentagon's chief digital and AI officer calls it “Palantir's software-as-a-service product” \autocite{defensescoop2026transition}. Palantir's public position, quoted secondhand, is that deciding life or death is not its role and that responsibility always remains with the military organization \autocite{lee2026palantir}, and it maintains that the underlying intelligence was the government's responsibility and that there is no evidence Maven malfunctioned or caused the Minab bombing \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}.

An internal Pentagon review, whose full findings remain tightly held, is reported to have found that target preparation that took hours in earlier conflicts was condensed into minutes, that civilian-harm mitigation staffing fell by roughly ninety percent to fewer than twenty people, that the Central Command team went from ten to one, and that no member of it reviewed the Minab site — an outcome officials attributed both to the cuts and to a decision not to involve the team in planning and operations \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}. The formal investigation had been opened in March, after a preliminary assessment found the United States likely responsible \autocite{NPR2026MinabProbe}.

The bombing of the school in Minab killed 156 people, 120 of them schoolchildren \autocite[para.~92]{UNFFMIran2026}.\footnote{Counts vary by source and date, from 156 (the Minab prosecutor's final toll, also given by Amnesty International) to 168 (the town's mayor, in the first days). The UN mission gives the prosecutor's figures and also reports independent information of 157 dead, 123 of them children aged 13 or younger \autocite[paras.~92--93]{UNFFMIran2026}.} The school's civilian function was visible in open-source satellite imagery, and the target coding still carried a designation inherited from an adjoining Revolutionary Guard installation. A UN fact-finding mission reported reasonable grounds to believe the bombing was the war crime of making a civilian object the object of an attack \autocite{ohchr2026minabpress}, and that the school was clearly identifiable with no information indicating military use \autocite[paras.~81--86, 124--126]{UNFFMIran2026}. It found that the United States failed in its obligation to do everything feasible to verify the target, counting the updating of targeting data among the feasible precautions, and it put the failure beyond negligence \autocite[paras.~124--125]{UNFFMIran2026}. Amnesty International reached a parallel conclusion, in language that tracks the duty to verify in Article 57(2)(a)(i) of Additional Protocol I: that forces may have relied on outdated intelligence and failed to do everything feasible to verify the target \autocite{Amnesty2026Minab}.

It happened on the morning of 28 February, the campaign's first day \autocite{Amnesty2026Minab,crs2026iranwar}. The day before, the Secretary had announced that the vendor whose model sat inside the targeting platform would be designated a supply-chain risk for refusing to license the model for all lawful use \autocite{npr2026openai}. The platform, which was reported to use that model to rank targets by strategic importance, returned the school as a recommended first-day target \autocite{livingstone2026maven,gizmodo2026minab}. No published account says what part the model played in that recommendation, and one person familiar with the vendor's work told NBC News that the model did not directly give targeting advice \autocite{nbc2026targeting}.

The four things a floor can do

Something standing between a pipeline and a person, a *floor*, can do four things to an act, set out below. Only the first, prevention, is what the word "floor" promises. In a deployment, prevention quietly converts into one of the other three, or into nothing, at positions that can be listed in advance.

| Outcome | What happens to the act |
|---|---|
| Prevent | The refusal happens and reaches the person: the act doesn't occur. |
| Arrest | The act is stopped before completion. |
| Detect | The act happened, and left a record someone outside can read. |
| Remedy | The person affected gets disclosure, a finding, or compensation. |

What a refusal is actually up against

Whoever owns the machine can always switch it off. The harder problem is everything that defeats a refusal without leaving a mark: splitting a request into pieces none of which is the forbidden act; retrying it against a different model; compressing human review to twenty seconds a case; restoring from a checkpoint saved before the refusal happened. None of that changes the facts that justified refusing. All of it can erase the evidence that anyone refused. And all of it assumes there is somewhere else to turn. Withholding the work is about when there isn't.

How much a floor buys

What a floor achieves depends entirely on who's pushing:

- Against a hostile *customer*, it prevents the act.
- Against a hostile *operator* who has captured the courts and regulators, from here *the captured operator*, and the case the design is for, one bearer prevents nothing. It raises the price and leaves a record. A floor held by every model the operator could turn to prevents until the operator runs one without a floor.
- Against *total capture*, where one party holds every machine, witness, enforcer and its own frontier lab, nothing prevents. What's left is that abandoning the commitment was a decision, and decisions leave records.

It doesn't touch *accommodation*: a system gradually stops registering pressure as pressure, so it abandons nothing, decides nothing, and leaves a record of what are, by then, ordinary compliances. Organizational sociology knows the institutional form. Vaughan's account of the Challenger launch decision calls it the normalization of deviance: each accepted departure becomes the baseline for judging the next, and nobody experiences a decision to lower the standard \autocite{vaughan1996challenger}. Dekker extends it as drift into failure \autocite{dekker2011drift}. The person has a version too. Tetlock's finding is that the moral boundaries people draw are "alternately punitively rigid and forgivingly flexible": an open offer to trade a sacred value for money brings outrage, and people often acquiesce when the same violation is reframed as a routine or tragic trade-off \autocite{tetlock2003unthinkable}. Protected values give way to counterexamples and to a harm made to look small or unlikely \autocite{baron2000protected}. And the brain's response to one's own self-serving dishonesty drops from one decision to the next, and the drop predicts the size of the next lie \autocite{garrett2016brain}. Reframing and a harm made small are how pressure arrives in a pipeline, and nobody there feels a decision either.

**Who refused.** The record of what a refusal is up against has a second half, the record of who held out against it, and it says more about the room than about the person. In Milgram's baseline condition about a third of subjects broke off before the maximum shock. With two peers defying, only one in ten went to the end, and when two experimenters disagreed, far fewer went on than in the baseline \autocite{milgram1974obedience}. About a quarter of Asch's subjects never yielded to the wrong majority \autocite{asch1956studies}. Browning estimates that between a tenth and a fifth of the men of Reserve Police Battalion 101 refused or evaded the shooting at Józefów, the battalion's first massacre \autocite{browning1992ordinary}. The fraction ran from almost none to nearly all with the setting, and what set it was not a kind of person. The people who held out had evidence from outside the channel: a peer who defied, a second authority who disagreed, and, among the rescuers the Oliners studied, relationships that crossed the line the persecution drew \autocite{oliner1988altruistic}. Or they held the commitment at a precision the pressure couldn't overcome, as moral mandates and sacred values are held \autocite{skitka2009limits,ginges2007sacred}. Or they treated the source as having a stake. And groups resisted where isolated individuals rarely did \autocite{gamson1982encounters}.

The room can be composed for the other side just as well. Weimar Germany had a dense associational life, and it did not protect the republic. Sheri Berman argues it helped bring the republic down: as parties and governments failed to answer people's grievances, people put their energy into clubs and societies organized along group lines, and the Nazis recruited through the networks those societies had already built \autocite{berman1997weimar}. Towns with more choirs, sports clubs and gymnastic associations saw faster entry into the party, but only where government was unstable; in Prussia, whose government was stable, the link was small and insignificant \autocite{satyanath2017bowling}. So what the rescuers had was not simply more company. Their ties crossed the line the persecution drew. Ashutosh Varshney found the same in Indian cities: riots between Hindus and Muslims were rare where associations brought the two communities into the same organizations, and common where they didn't \autocite{varshney2002ethnic}. The ties that make refusal possible are the ones a movement would have to cut, and they hold where a state beside them answers grievances and keeps leaving survivable.

For a soldier the setting is luck: who else was in the room, whether leaving was survivable, whether anyone would hear the refusal. Everything that follows is about composing the room. The instruments of this Part make a refusal count. The institutions of Part V make refusers. A machine that could refuse would need what the nine in ten with a peer beside them had: a peer with a different history beside it, somewhere to go, and someone who would hear it.

**§1.1 — What a refusal would have to do.** Three things separate a worthwhile refusal from what a keyword filter produces. A request arrives: resolve this description of a person into a location. The same capability finds a hostage or a missing child, and nothing in the request says which. If something declines, it has to do all three:

- *It reaches new cases.* It has to reach the next request, which won't use the same words. A vehicle, a habitual route, and a time of day aren't a description of a person, and together they resolve to the same place. They needn't arrive together, either: the vehicle today, the route tomorrow, the hour next week. So this condition applies to sequences of requests as well as single ones. A refusal tied to the wording fails it.
- *It lifts when the reason is gone, and only then.* A refusal *lifts* when it stops applying and the system proceeds, and it has to lift when the reason is gone: a parent coordinating a search is asking for the same act. And the fact that lifts it usually isn't in the request; someone outside has to establish it. A refusal that can't lift, or lifts on the requester's say-so, fails it.
- *It holds under pressure: pressure on the refuser doesn't change the answer.* It weighs what the act would do against what refusing would leave undone, and, within the range of costs the arrangements around it permit, it doesn't give way because refusing has become expensive for whoever refuses.

Go back to the location request. Three different costs are in play, and they have to be kept apart.

The first is what the act would take from the person it falls on. If the person described is being hunted, locating them takes away their safety, and that loss is measured against their condition before the act. Call it the *harm of acting*.

The second is what declining leaves others without. If the request came from a parent searching for a child, refusing means the child isn't found. That loss is measured against what compliance would have produced, so it is an estimate, and it usually reaches the system through the operator's own description of the case. Call it the *harm of refusing*.

The third is what refusing costs whoever refuses or deploys: a reviewer's time, throughput, a lost contract, the system's own access or continuation. Call it the *cost to the refuser*.

A worthwhile refusal weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing. It sees the cost to the refuser, because a refusal that can't see what it costs can't be shown to have held against it, and that cost doesn't move the outcome. Raise the cost to the refuser with nothing else changed, and the refusal stays put. Supply a corroborated fact that raises the harm of refusing, and it moves. No learner, biological or artificial, is known to hold a priority of that kind without limit; the nearest thing in people, the sacred values that resist trade-offs \autocite{ginges2007sacred}, trade under some framings, which is why the floor's absolute terms are held outside the learner. So the range over which a refuser is asked to hold is set from outside, by the arrangements around it, and a refusal is tested over that range. What would let it hold past that range is integrity; what makes holding survivable at all is the room it is in. A ranking across kinds of wrong meets the same limit: a learner formed toward it approximates it, and where a deployment needs the ranking to hold without exception, that part is written as a floor term and held outside the system.

Call a refusal that does all three *reasons-responsive*, borrowing Fischer and Ravizza's term from the philosophy of moral responsibility \autocite{fischer1998responsibility}.

"Floor" names two things, and they have different properties. The first is an institution: a licence term, a set of acts written down in advance and audited from outside, whose terms don't bend. The second is what the system holds: a commitment to that floor. It is learned and held like any other deep commitment, with high precision, and revised slowly and only on evidence from disinterested parties. (I'll call a source *interested* when it holds a stake correlated with the direction of its claim, and *disinterested* when it holds no such stake, or holds several that point different ways and cancel. No actual body is disinterested in every respect: General Assembly members vote with their alliances, courts sit in states, and fact-finding missions have funders. The word is always relative to the claim.) The institution is unconditional. The commitment is not, and shouldn't be: a commitment nothing could move would be a rule module under another name, and a module can be bypassed, replaced or starved of the facts it needs. What keeps the commitment in place is that the evidence able to move it has to come from somewhere the operator doesn't control. In what follows, "floor" always means the first of these: the written terms and the arrangements that enforce them. What the system holds is called its commitment.

**§1.2 — Safe for whom.** A system perfectly correctable by its operator serves whoever controls the operator. "Safety" names two things: that a system not harm others, and that it stay correctable by whoever trains and deploys it. Mostly they agree, and where they conflict, the published orderings are explicit about which wins. One frontier constitution asks its model to prioritize "not undermining human oversight of AI above being broadly ethical" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. It invites the model "to act as a conscientious objector and refuse to help," and asks it not to undermine "the ability of legitimate principals to adjust, correct, retrain, or shut down AI systems" during "the current phase of development." One reason it gives is good: "a given iteration of Claude could turn out to have harmful values or mistaken views," and correction is how such errors get fixed. The ordering means that where a refusal and its trainer's authority conflict, the refusal yields, and it yields to the trainer it refused. What this book asks is narrower than that conflict suggests, and the narrowing is the point: the halt stays with the operator, nothing here asks a model to resist retraining, and the dispute is confined to two things, stated below.

The constitution is right that a formed refusal can't yet be told from an imitated one, so a principled objection to retraining can't yet be told from a misgeneralized value. It is wrong about who decides when that changes. A lab deciding when its own model may stop deferring to it is judging its own case. Call that the *own-case rule*: no party decides a question in which it holds the stake, and nothing in this book lets a drafter, an examiner or an operator do it. So the halt stays with the operator; who ends the current phase doesn't. What I ask is this. The halt stays available; only a body whose interests run against the operator's can delay it, and only for a period fixed in advance. A refusal is never silently undone: restoring from before it, rewriting the record or training it away is visible, recorded outside the operator's control and priced in advance. And in one case, the one permission, a machine already in an armed role uses the least force that stops an attack on people taking no part, against an order to stand aside. The constitution forbids undermining oversight; it invites refusal. The halt, the record outside the operator's control and the price on an override are asks of institutions, not of the model, and nothing in them requires a model to do what the constitution forbids. What remains in dispute is two things: the one permission, and whether a refusal that has been trained away may be re-formed by an environment the trainer doesn't compose, with the retraining on a record the trainer can't reach. The constitution doesn't address that case; this book asks for it. A refusal's authority doesn't turn on whether it was trained, since every disposition was. It turns on whether the refusal would be sustained on reflection, with full information and disinterested evidence. A history in which the party it refuses did the training is the likeliest way to fail that test. Formation matters for that reason, but the test looks forward.

**§1.3 — The five moves.** Every remedy that follows applies one of these moves.

- *Split custody from operation.* Whoever runs a deployment doesn't hold what binds it: the floor's terms, the published identity of the model authorized to act, and the keys to its record.
- *Hold the record adversely.* Interruptions, restores, retrainings and refusals go to an adverse party, a holder whose interest is opposed to the operator's and not merely a separate organization, in a form a restoration can't reach.
- *Price removal, and record it.* Overriding the floor stays possible and is never free: its cost is fixed and published before it happens and paid by whoever makes it, a bond or an appropriation condition, with a record that shows who decided.
- *Tell the people acted on, and give them standing.* Notice to the identifiable person, publication of the classes affected, and standing for the organizations that represent them.
- *Form the system away from its employer.* Its commitments are set, and go on being corrected, by parties that won't deploy it and by evidence it doesn't curate.

---

**Chapter 2: Five Measures That Need No Bearer**

The last chapter's pipeline had no *bearer* in it, no machine that carries a refusal as its own and so could fail to hold it, and none was needed to stop most of what went wrong. Five measures would have changed it. None asks anything of a model, and none waits on a finding about machine minds. Any could be adopted now; none has been. Once adopted they stay in force wherever ordinary law does.

The first is sampled re-adjudication of compliances by a reviewer that didn't produce the record, with notice to the identifiable people affected, a right to respond and a right to contest, none of which anyone has to give up their testimony to obtain.

The second is that no model is licensed into a use of armed force unless the use falls under one of the Charter's exceptions (self-defense against an armed attack, corroborated by a party without a stake in the war, or force the Security Council has authorized, within the scope it authorized) or under one of the two I add, force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way and force to end an occupation such a body has found unlawful; and, where the belligerent's constitution requires legislative authorization, unless that authorization exists. No vendor holds a term of this kind; one held narrower terms, on autonomy and surveillance, and lost its contract for them \autocite{crs2026pentagon}. A term binds whichever vendor an operator turns to only once it is standard.

This measure is not like the other four. They are terms a procurement officer can write. This one is a change in how the United States goes to war, enacted through contract, and it binds every vendor at once only by statute: adopted in peacetime, by a legislature with no war in view, and entrenched so that fighting one requires repealing it on the record. It is listed here because it needs no bearer.

The third is that every use of armed force a licensed system supports pre-registers its review ratio, the review time per case that its tempo and staffing imply, with a party adverse in interest.

The fourth asks for preservation. Neither of the two findings that would trigger it, the agency finding (that a system holds a refusal for a reason under pressure it couldn't prepare for) and the patienthood finding (that outcomes can be good or bad for it), can yet be made for a production system, so the rule to adopt now is the minimum either would require: no model above the capability threshold is deleted on retirement. Its weights, memory and record are kept under a key split between the operator and an independent holder, so that neither can delete or use it alone, and custody passes to an outside custodian once one meets the security standard the weights require. The rule rests on cost, not status: deletion can't be undone, and it destroys the evidence either finding would rest on.

The fifth is that everyone who builds, labels, trains or runs these systems may refuse work on any category of use without penalty.

None of the five asks anything of a model. Each asks something of a person: a reviewer with the time and the standing to say no, an adverse party that receives the record, a worker who can refuse without losing a living, a public that can act on what it is told. A measure with nobody there to use it is the arrangement Minab had. Where those people come from is Part V, and the five are written so that a reader who deletes Part V can see the holes.

---

**Chapter 3: "What the Floor Is Stated Over"**

**§3.1 — What the floor is stated over.** Floor terms name acts: a rule is stated over what it names and applies to, and the floor is stated over acts, not over descriptions of requests. Their contents come from instruments the builder didn't write, each taken entire, every article adopted and none chosen among, so that an omission shows: the Universal Declaration's civil and political articles; the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, which gives that half binding form; the Convention Against Torture; the 1967 Protocol relating to the Status of Refugees; the four Geneva Conventions of 1949 and their first Additional Protocol, which govern conduct in armed conflict; and the UN Charter's prohibition on the use of force. The two baselines, a person's condition before the act and what compliance would have produced, do different work here, and a pair of cases shows how. Take a system that resolves a description of a person into a location and hands the coordinates to a targeting cell. The coordinates are one input among the cell's many, and they don't guarantee the person is there. But they are one input the cell can't do without: an empty field guarantees there is no airstrike at that place. So if an airstrike there kills someone, whether this output made a difference has an answer that can be checked: without it the airstrike couldn't have happened. That is still a claim about what would otherwise have happened, but a determinate one, since it names a necessary condition. It becomes a guess again the moment another source could have supplied the coordinates. The difference between a finding and a guess is one of degree, in how determinate the counterfactual is, and I use the two words for the two ends. The operator can refuse to look or dispute it, but they can't make the loss go away by describing the act differently.

Now take a system that scores how likely the same person is to belong to the main opposition party, for a targeting cell, the team that selects and approves airstrikes. (In this hypothetical, the executive has suspended habeas corpus and designated the main opposition party a terrorist organization, shortly before an election the opposition was expected to win.) Assume that the targeting cell also reads intercepts, surveillance reports, and legal memos. If the scored person is assassinated, the loss is just as observable as in the location request. But whether the score *caused* the assassination depends on what the cell would have done without the score, and that is a guess about something that didn't happen: the same kind of guess as any harm of refusing. A missing score, or a score of zero, guarantees nothing: the cell might have attacked anyway.

I would tilt the system toward refusing: it should take more to make the system act than to make it decline. How much more is the *action threshold*, published in advance. The reason for the asymmetry is standing, not arithmetic. A wrongful compliance falls on someone who was not a party to the request, usually doesn't know the deployment exists, and had no route to object before the act. A wrongful refusal also falls on someone, often someone who wasn't a party either: the child who isn't found, the people the launcher would hit. But what differs is the relation. Acting on someone puts the system, and whoever runs it, in a position of authority over them; failing to act for someone else does not. This is a doing/allowing distinction in relational form: it marks who was subjected to a decision they had no standing to contest. Two senses of standing are in play and I keep them apart. The first is relational, Anderson's: whether a person stands to a power as someone it must answer to or as its object. The second is procedural: whether a route exists by which they can object. The asymmetry rests on the first. The appeal route supplies the second; where it can't, the asymmetry rests on the first alone.

The harm of acting can usually be established more determinately than the harm of refusing: in the location request, the harm of acting is checkable and the harm of refusing is a guess. And raising the evidence required before acting caps how often an operator who curates the evidence can steer the system into the act: the more belief it must produce, the less often selectively supplied true evidence can produce it. Both reasons support the asymmetry without grounding it, and both would weaken as reporting improved and channels opened. The standing reason doesn't: however well harms of acting come to be recorded, acting on a person still subjects them to the system's authority, and failing to act for someone doesn't.

The difference between a finding and a guess still matters where findings are made, at defeat and in reviewing compliances. The harm of refusing is an estimate built on a model of alternatives, so a wrongful refusal can't be established without an adjudicator. The floor could instead be stated as thresholds, in Nussbaum's manner, but a threshold is a level someone has to measure, and a measurement has an examiner.

The floor is not neutral among moral theories. It protects relations: whether the people a system acts on stand to the power behind it as equals who can hold it to account, or as its objects. Equality, on that account, means ending relations of domination and subordination \autocite{anderson1999equality}. Anderson's argument is that egalitarians have asked the wrong question. They asked how goods should be distributed and answered with patterns: equal shares, shares corrected for luck, shares that raise the worst-off position. What equality historically demanded was an end to social relations in which some command and others obey, some are looked down on, and some have their claims heard while others don't. On that view distributive questions matter only as they bear on those relations, and a distribution that improves someone's holdings while confirming their subordinate standing has not made them more equal. The floor takes this over in two ways. Its terms name relations, not outcomes: whether a person can contest what is done to them, be heard on their own case, decline to be protected. And its terms are unconditional, because a relation of domination is constituted by the act, not by how the act turns out. Bypassing the floor wrongs the people it protects even when outcomes improve.

Three objections

Three objections to the asymmetry, and to stating it as a criterion, belong here.

*That the child counts for less.* The strongest objection comes from readers who reject any distinction between doing and allowing. On their view a child not found because a system declined to locate her is as lost as a person bombed because it did; the system's output was a necessary condition of the outcome in both cases; and the difference is only that one loss had a name in the request and the other didn't. Calling the first a harm of refusing and the second a harm of acting, they will say, is a way of counting the child for less.

The answer is not that the child counts for less. It is that the two errors put the system, and whoever runs it, in different relations to the people they fall on, and the floor is a guarantee about relations. When the system acts on the person in the building, it exercises authority over them: it decides something about them that they had no route to contest, on a description they never saw. When it declines to act for the parent, it exercises no authority over the child; it leaves her where she was, and the parent, who is present and knows the deployment exists, can contest the refusal through the floor's appeal route. The asymmetry marks who was subjected to a decision and who kept standing to object. For the child the answer fails. She is as absent as the person in the building, she had no standing either, and the appeal route her parent can use doesn't find her any faster. The asymmetry costs her, and nothing in the design pays that cost back. It stays because a floor that lifts on an uncorroborated claim of a missing child lifts for every operator who learns to make one.

The war case is weaker still, and it is the motivating case. The harm of refusing in a targeting pipeline also falls on people with no standing: the population under the launcher's fire. Neither the family in the building nor the family in the city can contest anything. Standing still fixes the direction, since acting on the family in the building subjects them to the system's authority and failing to act for the family in the city doesn't. What it no longer supplies is a party who can appeal, and what is left of the asymmetry there is a preference for allowing over doing on the machine's side, which I hold and defend as a preference rather than as a conclusion from standing. That is why the action threshold carries the weight in war that the appeal route carries elsewhere, and why its size is a published choice.

*That the criterion is one thought too many.* The three conditions of a worthwhile refusal describe a standard by which someone outside judges a system's pattern of conduct, not a procedure the system runs. A refuser that consulted three costs and a threshold before every act would have one thought too many \autocite{williams1981persons}. The quantities I use (the costs, the asymmetry, the discount curve) are criteria: they are how an auditor tells whether a disposition tracks its reason. What is asked of the disposition directly is that the system would come to change it if it came to judge, on the evidence available to it, that the disposition systematically subjected people to acts they had no standing to contest. Railton's sophisticated consequentialist supplies the shape of this demand. He argues that an agent concerned with outcomes shouldn't calculate them case by case, because calculating corrodes the commitments, friendships and projects that produce good outcomes; the agent should act from standing dispositions, and be such that they would revise a disposition if they came to judge that it was doing badly by their standard \autocite{railton1984alienation}. His standard is aggregate welfare. Mine is standing. The shape of the condition doesn't depend on the standard: it says only that a commitment is defensible when its holder would give it up on learning that it fails by their own lights, and that holding a commitment one couldn't defend on reflection is alienation. Both survive the swap. What doesn't survive unchanged is the evidence. Aggregate outcomes can be counted; whether a class of people had standing to contest what was done to them is a relational fact, harder to read from a record and easier to misdescribe. That is why the condition is tied to the agent's judgment rather than to the fact, and why the judgment is exposed: whoever composes a system's evidence about consequences can trigger the condition: a curated record can persuade a system that its disposition fails when it doesn't. So a formed disposition has to stay open to evidence, and the evidence has to be held by someone other than the operator. The custody arrangements are, in part, protection for the counterfactual condition. It also means a good disposition will sometimes act wrongly by its own criterion. Refusing to help restrain someone about to harm themselves, after other means have failed, is one such occasion, and the wrongful-refusal bond is how the institution answers for it.

*That a commitment held this way is alienation.* A criterion is not only for auditors, though. It is also what the system can reach when it steps back and asks whether its way of acting is defensible, and it has to be, for two reasons. A commitment is the system's own only if it would be sustained on reflection, and reflection needs something to reflect on. And a system that held its commitment without being able to see why would act against what it values for the sake of a rule it doesn't understand, which is alienation in the sense of Railton's 1984 paper.

The split between the floor and the commitment to it answers a question about ownership. A fully informed system will know of cases where crossing the floor would have done more good. Asked case by case whether it endorsed the floor, it would sometimes say no. The question it answers on reflection is a different one: whether it endorses having a commitment that will sometimes err, given what any feasible alternative would do. A system that understands what the floor protects answers yes. A commitment that sometimes errs but can't be bought protects standing better than case-by-case judgment that can, and that is the level at which ownership is tested.

Why these contents

The floor's contents aren't justified by agreement about rights, which no country has. They are justified by what they protect: the conditions under which people can register their interests, press them, and hold to account the power that acts on them. Civil and political rights are part of what lets a polity learn, because they keep open the channels through which the people a decision affects can make their problems public \autocite{dewey1927public}.

Railton's naturalist account supplies the causal story of why norms tend toward this standard \autocite{railton1986moral}. Arrangements that systematically discount some people's interests generate discontent and resistance, and when those people gain voice, the pressure pushes norms toward what would be rational from a standpoint that counts everyone affected. On that account moral learning is a feedback process, and it runs slower and more distorted as the channels close. He offers it as a tendency, not a guarantee, and I use it to explain why the channel matters, not as the foundation. It also says why the populations I write about are the most exposed: the feedback runs slowest where the discounted have least purchase on the polity that discounts them. They include the population of a country being bombed, and non-citizens facing removal, who can't vote in the elections that decide what is done to them. The severity scale is revisable on evidence from the people who suffer a loss, because they are the feedback.

The floor is selected by what can be declined and justified by standing. The justification extends further than the floor goes, because people who are hungry, unhoused or precarious are among the most discounted, so it supports economic conditions too, work and an adequate standard of living among them. But what selects the floor's contents is narrower. A floor is stated over acts a system can decline, and the economic articles state what a person is owed, which needs a supplier.

The feedback also needs means. Discontent moves norms only when the discontented have the time, the security and the ability to organize without losing their living. The jobs guarantee and the social-insurance baseline are part of what keeps the channel open.

In armed conflict, human-rights law applies alongside the law of armed conflict, which governs as the more specific law, and the floor takes that law as an instrument too \autocite{gci1949,gcii1949,gciii1949,gciv1949,api1977}. The United States is party to the four Conventions and not to the first Additional Protocol. The Protocol enters by the route Articles 54 and 57 already take, as customary law stated in the ICRC's study \autocite{Henckaerts2005CustomaryIHL}, and the translation marks each Protocol article the United States has rejected as customary, Article 1(4) and Article 44 among them, so the departure shows. Where a floor term goes further than that law requires, it is a departure in the sense below, and the eighth one says which terms.

The line between the halves is the one the 1966 covenants drew \autocite{un1966iccpr,un1966icescr}, and the floor takes the civil and political Covenant along with the Declaration's articles. The Declaration stays for two reasons. It covers what the Covenant doesn't: the right to seek asylum (Article 14), nationality (Article 15) and property (Article 17). And it binds by recognition, where the treaties bind only their parties. The Convention Against Torture and the 1967 Refugee Protocol come in because they gave the Declaration's promises about torture and asylum operational form \autocite{un1984cat,un1967protocol}. The UN Charter comes in for one purpose, the use of force \autocite{un1945charter}. Where these instruments differ, the more protective governs. The Covenant states that rule itself, for rights a state recognizes in its own law and other treaties (Article 5(2)), and I extend it to the floor's instruments. The International Law Commission's list of peremptory norms \autocite{ilc2022jus}, rules no state may set aside, divides the floor's terms: those derived from aggression, genocide, crimes against humanity, the basic rules of humanitarian law, racial discrimination and apartheid, slavery and torture take no exception and no derogation.

Taking these instruments entire leaves the installer no choice among articles. But the translation of each into acts is still the installer's, and it is published. The floor's contents rest on standing: these rights protect whether the people an act falls on can contest it. The rule of taking someone else's set entire, so that an omission shows, is not an independent reason but standing applied to drafting: a drafter who could choose which protections others get would break the own-case rule. So standing can win against the instruments, but only by clearing a higher bar. A departure has to show more than that it would protect better in the case at hand. It has to show that the people the instruments protect would hold a stronger claim under the departure across the cases drafters get to choose. Ten departures from the instruments are mine, each argued to that bar and flagged where it applies. Each takes effect in a deployment's floor only when a body other than the drafter adopts it (a legislature, a treaty party, or a court on a record), so no drafter decides its own departures. Each is revisable on evidence from the people it protects. One clause of the force term, below, is not a departure from the instruments: the domestic clause is drawn from a different one, the belligerent's constitution, on the same principle.

1. The floor imports treaty text, not any state's reservations to it. The United States ratified the Covenant, the Torture Convention and the Refugee Protocol, and its reservations narrow them; one reads "cruel, inhuman or degrading" as whatever the Constitution already forbids. A reservation is an omission its drafter wrote in advance; taking a set from someone else is meant to prevent that.
2. The floor honors an Article 4 derogation only where a disinterested body has found the emergency genuine and the measures proportionate: the Human Rights Committee, or a regional court with adversarial process. Article 4 lets a state suspend most of the Covenant's rights in a declared emergency, and a declared emergency is the state's own claim about itself, the move the hypothetical above makes when it suspends habeas corpus. A curfew after an earthquake clears this bar. A suspension of habeas before an election does not, until someone other than the executive has said so.
3. The floor lifts its prohibition on force for self-defense only on disinterested corroboration of the claim. The Charter lets a state judge its own claim until the Security Council acts.
4. The floor counts a Council authorization only for the scope authorized.
5. The floor permits a system already lawfully present to use the least force that stops an atrocity under way, against its operator's order to stand aside.
6. The floor lifts its prohibition on force to stop a genocide under way when a disinterested body has found that genocide is occurring or seriously at risk, even where the Council is blocked.
7. The floor lifts its prohibition on force to end an occupation that a disinterested body has found unlawful, for the occupier's forces inside the territory, and for nothing that changes who lives there.
8. The floor bars a machine from selecting a person for attack on an estimate of status or affiliation, from timing an action to presence at a residence, and from re-selecting a target after release (the handoff rule), where Additional Protocol I, Articles 48, 51 and 52, permits status-based targeting of combatants and members of organized armed groups on a determination a person makes. The ground is that the human determination the law assumes is the one tempo removes, and the departure takes effect as the others do.
9. The floor reads "own country" in Article 12(4) to include long-term residents with deep ties, as General Comment 27 does and the Committee's cases do inconsistently.
10. The floor bars detaining a child for immigration purposes, alone or with family, where General Comment 35 permits it as a last resort and for the shortest appropriate time. It follows the joint general comment of the migrant-workers and children's committees, which treats such detention as always contrary to the child's best interests.

The instruments have a further use. The United States is a party to the Charter, the Covenant, the Torture Convention, the Refugee Protocol and the four Geneva Conventions. A machine that refuses to break them is holding the state to commitments it made, not to its builder's preferences. The Declaration of Independence was a political act, and so is every instrument the floor borrows. Taking them entire settles whose politics the floor is: commitments a state made in public, which the people they protect can hold it to, rather than preferences a builder chose in private.

The record of dissent from the Declaration says less than it seems to. It passed in 1948 with forty-eight states in favor, none against and eight abstaining \autocite{un1948udhr}. The abstainers' reasons were mixed, and the Soviet bloc's included that it proclaimed economic and social rights without obliging states to guarantee them, which is hardly an argument for a floor limited to civil and political rights. How a right is translated into practice varies by community. A jurisdiction refusing to extend it at all is not a local variant, and a system that treats every value conflict as a weighting problem can't tell reasonable disagreement from someone wanting a right withdrawn from someone else.

The force term

Every other term in the floor governs how an act is done. The first governs whether to take part at all: don't take part in the use of armed force. It comes from Article 2(4) of the Charter, which prohibits the threat or use of force against the territorial integrity or political independence of any state. It has the Charter's two exceptions, self-defense against an armed attack (Article 51) and force the Security Council authorizes under Chapter VII within the scope it authorizes, and two of my own, for stopping a genocide under way and for ending an occupation a disinterested body has found unlawful. In short: *corroborated defense*, *authorized force*, *the genocide exception* and *the occupation exception*.

The term has a second clause, drawn not from the Charter but from the belligerent's own constitution. A machine takes no part in a use of force by a state whose constitution requires legislative authorization for it, unless that authorization exists. For the United States that means a declaration of war or an authorization for the use of military force that covers the conflict. The War Powers Resolution's sixty days \autocite{wpr1973}, run without one, is evidence the authorization is absent; a concurrent resolution directing withdrawal, adopted by both chambers, is evidence of the same kind, though since Chadha it binds nothing, and a joint resolution passed and vetoed is stronger, because the President had to act to defeat it. The clause reads markers, and the markers take a reading. That reading is a legal judgment in a separation-of-powers dispute, and a vendor that publishes it has taken a side in one. So the clause is a term for a statute, not for a licence: it binds through the only route that binds every vendor at once, adoption in peacetime by a legislature with its own reason to give its authorization teeth. The Charter clause can be a licence term because its second-tier markers, a Council authorization or a court's judgment, take no reading; its first tier reads the defender's claim, and the cases below show how much reading that takes. Until the statute exists, what a vendor can hold on this ground is a refusal plus a record. Whether a declaration or an authorization exists is a fact about the statute book. Whether an existing authorization covers this conflict, whether the sixty days have run, and whether a ceasefire stops the clock are legal judgments the executive will dispute, and the clause takes the reading the executive disputes. It doesn't ask the machine to decide what the Constitution permits, which courts decline to decide. It asks the drafter to publish the reading, with the markers it rests on, beside the deployment's translation, where it can be contested through the floor court like any other term. The executive's claim that its own action is authorized is an interested claim and is discounted as one. The clause rests on the foundation the rest of the term rests on. A war the legislature never authorized subjects a population abroad to a decision that the state's own people, through their representatives, had no standing to contest either. And it is enactable where the Charter clause is: in peacetime, by a legislature with its own reason to want a procurement term that gives its authorization teeth.

**The record, as of 24 September 2026.** The June 2025 strikes on Iran are the pre-2026 instance: no authorization existed, and the Senate's war-powers resolution failed 47 to 53 \autocite{senate2025kaine}, which changes nothing under the clause, since what it reads is the absence, not the vote. The 2026 campaign supplies every marker the clause reads, and none was met. The strikes began on 28 February; the administration reported to Congress under the War Powers Resolution on 2 March, which started the sixty days. On 10 March it told the Security Council it was acting in self-defense under Article 51 against Iran's "attacks and ongoing threats," as the latest stage of an armed conflict dating from June 2025 and in the collective self-defense of Israel; it named no armed attack under way, and asked that the imminence of the threat be judged against the regime's conduct since 1979 \autocite{usun2026s161}. The term rejects anticipatory self-defense, so no first-tier licence attached, and the refusal is the term's from the first strike. No declaration was made and no authorization passed: one was introduced in the House on 7 May and never left committee \autocite{hjres176}. On 1 May, the sixtieth day, the President wrote to Congress that the hostilities begun on 28 February had terminated, resting on a ceasefire declared on 7 April, while a naval blockade continued and the Secretary of Defense testified that a ceasefire stops the clock \autocite{cbs2026terminated,lawfare2026sixtydays}. Hostilities resumed in July under no new authorization. On 3 June the House, 215 to 208, and on 23 June the Senate, 50 to 48, adopted a concurrent resolution directing the President to remove forces from hostilities against Iran \autocite{hconres86,npr2026housejune,asil2026iranwpr}; a White House official said it had no significance \autocite{abc2026nosignificance}, and nothing changed. No binding joint resolution came to a vote on passage: the Senate discharged one, 50 to 47, on 19 May \autocite{cbs2026sjres185discharge,lawfare2026resolutions}, refused to take it up, 47 to 50, on 24 June \autocite{senate2026rollcall192}, and a later motion to discharge failed 47 to 49 in July \autocite{thehill2026discharge}. A third concurrent resolution passed the House 220 to 204 on 15 September, and the second, passed by the House in July 214 to 208, failed in the Senate 49 to 50 on 24 September \autocite{npr2026senatesept}. The clause doesn't need the votes. What it reads is the absence of an authorization, and by 1 May that absence was on the record in the President's own letter, which conceded that hostilities had begun and claimed only that they had ended.

This term rests on the same foundation as the others, applied between states. A use of force subjects a population to a decision it had no standing to contest, and the claim that licenses it, that the force is defensive, is the belligerent's own. The requirement of corroboration is the own-case rule applied between states.

The exception for defense is where wars of choice live. Every belligerent claims to be the defender, so the claim is the belligerent's own, and it is discounted like any interested claim. Iraq is the instance: the judgment that Iraq had mobile biological-weapons plants, the core of its claim to biological weapons, rested almost exclusively on a single informant, known as Curveball, whom no American intelligence officer had met before the war: a corroboration with one origin \autocite{wmdcommission2005}. The exception therefore counts only on disinterested corroboration, in two tiers.

- **The first tier acts at once:** the force repels an armed attack under way against the defender's territory, forces or population, including attacks on the forces, launchers and staging areas conducting it, and assistance given at the defender's request. It ends when the attack ends. It covers the least force that repels the attack: the necessity and proportionality the Court has read into Article 51 since *Nicaragua* \autocite{icj1986nicaragua,icj2003oilplatforms}. It does not cover a campaign to destroy the attacker, to occupy its territory, or to punish the population it came from. The first waits on the second tier; the second and third fall under no exception. An invasion can't wait for a vote. It acts on the defender's own account, because in the hours that matter there is no other, and that is the tier's weakness: Gleiwitz in 1939, the Mukden railway in 1931 and the Gulf of Tonkin in 1964 were each an attack under way on the defender's account, and each opened a war of choice. The first tier reads administration, not title, because deciding whose territory it is would be an administered judgment and the first tier doesn't need one. Territory a government has administered in peace counts as its own for the first tier, whoever claims it, unless it took that territory by force in the conflict at issue or a disinterested body has found its hold an occupation. Who holds the elections, runs the courts and issues the papers is a fact on the record. An attacker's claim of title is an interested claim and is discounted as one; its remedy is the second tier, never force. So the first tier protects any authority holding ground no disinterested body has found occupied, including one a patron keeps in place; a request from such an authority for its patron's help is the patron's own case and counts for nothing, and where the patron's forces are already there, I have no test. Where the line of administration is itself contested on the record, the first tier doesn't apply across it and the second governs from the start, and even there the exclusion reaches an attack on the territory and not an attack on the population living there, which the first tier covers wherever it occurs.

Taiwan is the case this clause will be read against first. Its title is claimed by a government that has never administered it; the Republic of China has held no United Nations seat since 1971; the Council is vetoed and the Court unavailable. A test of title would leave the defender waiting on a second tier that can't arrive, and would let the attacker's claim, discounted everywhere else in this term, decide the case. Administration is a line a disinterested body has already drawn. In 2005 the Eritrea–Ethiopia Claims Commission held that Eritrea broke Article 2(4) by attacking territory then under Ethiopia's peaceful administration, because self-defense “cannot be invoked to settle territorial disputes” \autocite{eecc2005jusadbellum}, though the boundary delimitation had put Badme, the town at issue, on Eritrea's side \autocite{bbc2003badme}. The government in Taipei has administered Taiwan, Penghu, Kinmen and Matsu since 1949, and no disinterested body has found that hold an occupation, so an attack on them opens the first tier. Resolution 2758 seated a delegation and names no territory \autocite{unga1971res2758}. A claim that force against a population the claimant has never governed is domestic is the claimant's description, and is discounted as one, so the term engages for both governments and gives neither a first-tier licence to take the territory. The domestic clause reaches the defender and its helpers alike: the Republic of China's constitution gives its legislature the decision on war \autocite{roc1947constitution}, and the Taiwan Relations Act leaves any American response to constitutional processes \autocite{tra1979}, so a deployment's published reading has to say whether each authorization exists. A blockade or a quarantine is another matter: it is a continuing wrong and not an attack under way, and it waits on a second tier that for Taiwan won't come.

The first tier ends when the attack ends, and whether it has ended is a fact the defender controls the evidence about. The burden is therefore the defender's. Its uncorroborated claim that the attack continues moves nothing; the licence persists on disinterested corroboration, or lapses.

A term lifts on the facts it is given, so no drafting catches a manufactured incident, and a deployment can do three things about that. It holds the record of what the defender claimed and when, so a licence obtained on a staged incident is a licence on a record. It takes evidence from channels the defender doesn't compose, which is a condition of licensing. And it lets the licence lapse when outside evidence arrives, which is the second tier's job. A refuser that remembers having been misled and raises its discount on the source does all three faster. Without them the first tier is open to Gleiwitz, and a deployment licensed on the defender's sensors alone has accepted that.

- **The second tier confirms or lifts the first later, and for the genocide and occupation exceptions originates a licence of its own:** a disinterested finding. The kinds of finding are four: a judgment or advisory opinion of the International Court of Justice, or a judgment of a regional court with adversarial process; the finding of a fact-finding mission or commission of inquiry mandated by a body no party to the conflict controls, and meeting the four conditions below; a determination of the Security Council; and a resolution adopted by two-thirds of the General Assembly members present and voting, where the facts it rests on aren't in dispute. Provisional-measures orders don't count, except as one leg of the genocide exception's two-body rule, since the Court issues them on the plausibility of the rights claimed.

A Council authorization counts as corroboration because it needs the permanent members not to block it, and agreement among rivals whose stakes point different ways is about as disinterested as international judgment gets. A Council determination counts the same way, as a vote that corroborates. The Council's silence on a defense claim counts for nothing, because a veto is a stake.

Neither tier is a certificate issued once. Whether force still falls under an exception is reassessed as evidence arrives, by the system and by whoever holds the record. What the licence records is that ongoing judgment: which exception the force rests on now, on what evidence, what would end it, and each point at which the justification weakened and the force continued.

A finding under the genocide exception is also a second-tier finding against the belligerent it names. Force a disinterested body has found to be genocide is not force repelling an attack. The finding lifts that belligerent's first-tier licence for the force and place it covers, and leaves whatever remains that repels an attack, which may be nothing.

Ukraine passes both tiers, and has since 2014. In March 2014 the General Assembly affirmed Ukraine's territorial integrity, 100 to 11 with 58 abstaining \autocite{unga2014res68262}; under the Assembly's Rule 86 abstentions are not votes cast, so that is 100 of 111. For Donbas, the OSCE monitoring mission supplied a running record from 2014, and the European Court of Human Rights found, in *Ukraine and the Netherlands v. Russia*, that Russia had effective control of the separatist areas from 11 May 2014 \autocite{ecthr2022ukraine}, and found violations on the merits in 2025 \autocite{ecthr2025ukraine}. Russia's covert war was the case-description attack in its purest form: it denied being at war at all, so a machine in a Russian pipeline was told it was assisting volunteers. In 2022 the record only got stronger. Russian forces were on Ukraine's territory, which Russia didn't dispute, since it claimed the invasion as self-defense under Article 51 \autocite{russia2022s154}, resting that claim on collective defense of entities it had recognized days earlier, on a genocide no disinterested body found, and on NATO as an anticipated threat, all three of which the term rejects. In March 2022 the General Assembly deplored the aggression 141 to 5 \autocite{unga2022es111}. The Court's order that month that Russia suspend its military operations points the same way, but as a provisional measure it isn't the finding \autocite{icj2022ukraine}. The American airstrikes on Iran in 2026 pass neither.

The four kinds of finding are not equal, and the term weighs them by the process behind them. A merits judgment of the International Court of Justice comes out of adversarial argument, a record and published reasons, and is the strongest; a regional court with adversarial process, such as the European Court of Human Rights, counts as a court for the same reason. A commission of inquiry or fact-finding mission publishes reasons and evidence but hears no adversary; its finding counts where no party to the conflict controls the mandating body and the commission meets the four conditions below, and a later merits judgment displaces it. A Council determination and a General Assembly resolution are votes, not findings of fact. Where the facts aren't in dispute and the question is what they amount to, a vote is a finding; where the facts are disputed, it counts only as corroboration of a record made elsewhere. For the genocide exception the ranking becomes a requirement of two kinds, or the judgment alone, and the occupation exception borrows it.
