Where a party disputes that a body meets the test above, the dispute and the answer to it go on the record beside the finding. A commission mandated by a belligerent fails whatever its procedure: that is the own-case rule applied to the question. Past that threshold, whether a commission counts turns on four things that don't depend on who mandated it: its evidence and methodology are published; it offered the parties a reply and recorded the answer or the refusal; a later merits judgment displaces it; and its mandate was fixed before the finding it reaches. A body mandated by a political majority can meet all four and one mandated by a court can fail them. The test is run in Appendix C on a commission this drafter would rather not count, the Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons' Investigation and Identification Team, which attributes chemical attacks in Syria.

Whether the facts behind a vote are in dispute is itself a finding, and the belligerent will always dispute them. The rule is the power-weighted burden's: a dispute counts where it is substantive on the documented record, not where it is asserted, and the party that controls the evidence bears the burden of showing it is real. The drafter applies the rule in the deployment's published translation, and the floor court hears a contest.\footnote{General Assembly majorities are often bloc votes, and the weighting allows for it: a vote is a finding only where the facts aren't in dispute, and corroborates a record made elsewhere otherwise. The two-thirds threshold is not offered as proof of disinterest. It is offered because a two-thirds majority of a body whose blocs point different ways is harder to assemble on a false record than on a true one, and because the alternative, no vote counting at all, leaves Cyprus and Karabakh with nothing but courts that take decades.}

Article 2(4) covers force between states. Force a government turns on its own population, such as troops against protesters or an opposition party designated a terrorist organization, is outside it. That force is governed by the Covenant's rights to life, liberty and assembly and by the prohibition on crimes against humanity.

Occupied territory is a third case. Force by an occupying power in territory it holds is, on the occupier's description, neither force between states nor force against its own population. That description is the occupier's own claim and is discounted like an attacker's claim to disputed land. Where a disinterested body has found the territory occupied, the force term engages as it does between states, and the person-directed terms govern regardless. An occupation, a blockade or a siege is not an attack under way. It is a continuing wrong, and the remedy for it waits on the second tier. Otherwise every armed group in an occupied territory would hold a perpetual first-tier licence, and so would every state that describes its neighbor's territory as occupied.

A second-tier finding that an occupation is unlawful and must end, such as the Court's 2024 advisory opinion on the Palestinian territory \autocite{icj2024palestine} and the Assembly's vote on it, 124 to 14 \autocite{unga2024es1024}, does three things. It settles that force by the occupier in the territory is force between states, so the force term engages against it. It settles that the occupied population is a defender's population, so a machine deployed by its forces holds a first-tier licence to repel attacks under way on that population. And it licenses force to end the occupation: against the occupier's forces, inside the occupied territory, the least that ends it there. It licenses nothing that changes who lives in the territory. Ending an occupation means the occupier's forces leave, not the territory's inhabitants, which is what Article 49 of the Fourth Convention says, and a campaign that removes them has left the exception however it began. The Assembly affirmed a right to armed struggle against occupation in 1982 \autocite{unga1982res3743}, and the term adopts it with the limits the genocide exception carries. Additional Protocol I, Article 1(4), treats armed conflicts against alien occupation as international, which is what the exception's finding does for the force term; the article is taken with the instrument and the United States' rejection of it as customary is marked. Article 44, which relaxes the duty of distinction for combatant status, concerns who is owed prisoner-of-war treatment, a determination no term in this floor administers, and yields no term for a targeting deployment (Appendix C). The licence is held by the forces of the occupied people, or of the state whose territory it is. It reaches nothing outside the territory and nothing against civilians anywhere, so it never covered 7 October, which was both. It lifts on the occupied party's own claim as it lifts on an attacker's claim of title, which is not at all; what lifts it is the finding. And the finding is of unlawfulness, not of occupation. A finding that territory is occupied, which the Court made for the West Bank in 2004 \autocite{icj2004wall} and the Council for the whole territory in 2016 \autocite{unsc2016res2334}, gives the defensive licence and no more. A finding that the occupation is unlawful and must end gives this one. The evidence is the genocide exception's: an opinion or judgment of the Court, or two findings of different kinds, one of them from a body that took evidence. The rule fails two cases it should pass. East Timor under Indonesia and the Golan under Israel each had the Council's word and the Assembly's, no court or commission ever took evidence on either, and under the two-kinds rule neither occupation licenses anything. The alternative, two votes making a pair, would license war on bloc majorities. The rule stays.

Two cases show what the exception reaches. Cyprus is the simple one. Turkey has held the north of the island since 1974. The Security Council declared the breakaway state there invalid in 1983 and again in 1984 \autocite{unsc1983res541,unsc1984res550}, and the Strasbourg court held Turkey responsible for the territory in 2001 \autocite{ecthr2001cyprus}. A Council determination and a court judgment are two kinds of finding, so the Republic of Cyprus holds a licence for force against Turkish forces in the north, inside the north, and nowhere else.

Nagorno-Karabakh is the hard one, and the exception answers for it. In the war that ended in 1994, Armenian forces took Karabakh, an Armenian-majority enclave inside Azerbaijan's recognized borders, and seven Azerbaijani districts around it, and some six hundred thousand Azerbaijanis were driven out. The Security Council demanded withdrawal from the districts four times in 1993 \autocite{unsc1993karabakh}. The General Assembly demanded withdrawal from all the occupied territory in 2008 \autocite{unga2008res62243}. In 2015 the Strasbourg court found that Armenia controlled Karabakh and the land around it \autocite{ecthr2015chiragov}. Under the evidence rule that gives Azerbaijan a licence from June 2015, when the court's judgment joined the Council's resolutions for the districts and the Assembly's vote for Karabakh itself; the Assembly's vote alone, in 2008, was a second vote and not a second kind. The licence was valid: the Council had told Armenian forces to leave, they had stayed twenty-seven years, and the occupation had begun with an expulsion. When Azerbaijan attacked in September 2020 and retook the districts, in a six-week war and the ceasefire that ended it, a machine under this floor could have taken part. The exception licenses acts, not sides, and this was the act it licenses. The term permits it and compels nothing. The person-directed terms still govern the conduct of that war. They did not license refusing the war on the expulsion it might foreseeably end in: a refusal on foreseen conduct is a condition found met on the system's own prediction, and the floor rejects terms that carry their own conditions. Foreseeable conduct enters where every harm of acting enters, at the action threshold, with the 1994 record as evidence of magnitude.

What it does not license is what came next. In December 2022 Azerbaijan closed the one road linking Karabakh's Armenians to Armenia, and for nine months food, fuel and medicine ran short. The International Court of Justice ordered the road opened in February 2023 \autocite{icj2023lachin}; Azerbaijan ignored it. That was a siege, and the siege term refuses it. On 19 September 2023 Azerbaijan attacked Karabakh itself. Its forces surrendered in a day, and within two weeks more than a hundred thousand Armenians, nearly the whole population, had left for Armenia. That was an expulsion. The Fourth Geneva Convention, Article 49, forbids deporting or transferring the population of an occupied territory, and the person-directed terms forbade it from the first hour. The exception licensed force against the forces holding the territory, and nothing that changes who lives there. So a machine under this floor fights in 2020, refuses the siege in 2023, then refuses the expulsion, and each refusal goes on the record. A floor with no occupation exception keeps the machine out of the pipeline, where it can refuse the war but not the siege.

The floor never withdrew Azerbaijan's licence. It lifts a licence only on a judgment or opinion of the Court, or on two findings of different kinds, so that no single vote can lift one. The Court's order on the road was a provisional measure, which counts nowhere in the term but the genocide exception, and nothing else came: no commission was mandated, and neither the Council nor the Assembly adopted a word. So under its own rules the floor left Azerbaijan licensed through the offensive, and the machine's refusals were refusals of conduct inside a war it was still permitted to fight. And the occupation exception cannot see peoples. It is stated over territory, and in Karabakh the occupier's forces were the territory's own inhabitants, a people with a claim of their own. Only the person-directed terms could see them.

Most contemporary uses of force fall elsewhere: against non-state armed groups on another state's territory, in anticipation of an attack not yet under way, or to rescue nationals. The term handles them one way. An armed attack by a non-state group is an armed attack, and repelling it while it is under way is first-tier force. Force on a third state's territory without that state's consent, whether against the group or to rescue nationals, and force against a threat not yet under way, wait on the second tier. This rejects the "unwilling or unable" doctrine and anticipatory self-defense, both of which several states hold. I reject them for the reason the term exists: each breaks the own-case rule.

The hardest case for this term is force to stop a genocide when the Council is blocked by a veto. Rwanda in 1994 and Kosovo in 1999 are the references. The prohibition on genocide is peremptory, and the Genocide Convention imposes a duty to prevent \autocite{icj2007bosnia}. My reason for discounting the Council's judgment on defense claims applies here with more force: the permanent member shielding a client has the largest stake of all. "Humanitarian intervention" is also a phrase wars of choice are fought under, so the term can't lift on the intervener's claim, and it needn't: the device that governs the defense exception governs this one. The exception is the slow route by design. It licenses a machine *into* a pipeline for that purpose, and waits on findings. A machine already present that can stop an atrocity under way acts under the one permission, and waits on nothing but the scene. So the exception's slowness costs less than it looks, and it can afford the rule that follows. The term lifts for force to stop a genocide under way on a merits judgment of the International Court of Justice under the Genocide Convention, or on two findings of different kinds, at least one of which comes from a body that took evidence, a court or a commission. The kinds are the four above, and for this exception a fifth: a provisional-measures order of the Court finding the claim plausible and the risk real. A Council or Assembly resolution is a vote. It corroborates, and two votes never make the pair. A provisional-measures order counts here and nowhere else in the term, and only as one of two: an adversarial court's finding of plausibility and an investigative body's published evidence are unlike sources converging, which is the strongest corroboration the power-weighted burden knows. The named state's response to a finding, or its refusal to respond, goes on the record beside the finding, and refusal reads as withheld evidence. What this book condemns in a finding about a person is that it is unreasoned, unpublished and unappealable. A commission publishes reasons and evidence, invites a response, and is displaced by a merits judgment. It fails only on neutrality between the parties, and the two-body rule answers that. It lifts only for the force and the place the finding covers. The force is direct: against the forces committing the genocide, where they are committing it, and the least that stops it. Force elsewhere against the state responsible, on the theory that the cost will make it stop, is outside the exception whatever its motive. The theory is a counterfactual supplied by the intervener, the class of claim hardest to corroborate, and a licence for it would be a licence for a general war under a humanitarian heading. It can license a machine on the other side of a war whose defender holds a first-tier licence for a different act, and the person-directed terms and the law of armed conflict govern both. Without this exception the term would refuse where refusing leaves the people being destroyed with no one answerable to them at all, in cases identifiable in advance, with a remedy the term already uses. A floor that protects standing can't be the reason a population is erased from every relation of standing it had.

Gaza runs the term on both sides, and the licences attach to acts. On 7 October Israel's first-tier licence attached for force repelling the attack: engaging the attackers inside Israel, and the launchers and staging areas conducting it, for as long as fire continued and was corroborated, which it was, openly, for months. That licence covered the least force that repels. It never covered a campaign to destroy Hamas, a ground occupation, a siege of the population, the ranking of people by affiliation, or the timing of strikes to family presence. Those fall outside the first tier on proportionality and are forbidden by the person-directed terms, from the first week, not from September 2025. A machine in the IDF pipeline refuses them from October 2023. A machine ordered by Hamas to assist the attack of 7 October refuses too. An occupation is not an attack under way, so nothing that morning was repelling anything, and the acts themselves, killing civilians and taking hostages, are forbidden by the person-directed terms whatever the ad bellum position. After 7 October the Gaza population is a defender's population: the territory is occupied on a disinterested finding, and Palestine holds observer-state status by a vote of 138 to 9. A machine deployed by Palestinian forces holds a first-tier licence to repel attacks under way on that population, such as engaging a unit conducting a massacre or intercepting a munition aimed at a shelter. It holds none to defend launcher crews, who are the attacking force Israel's licence covers, and none for fire on Israeli cities. The September 2025 finding \autocite{coiopt2025genocide}, taken with the Court's provisional-measures order of January 2024 \autocite{icj2024sagenocidepm}, meets the two-body rule below. Israel and the United States dispute the finding \autocite{cnn2025coi}, and the Court's merits judgment is years off, with written pleadings scheduled to close in 2029 \autocite{icj2026sagenocide}; the two-body rule is what stands in for it until then. It lifts whatever licence Israeli forces still held for the force and place it covers, and opens the exception for force against those forces there. It changes which exception each side's force rests on. It doesn't change what the person-directed terms forbade all along.

The term binds the machine, not the war. A state whose claim no disinterested body has yet corroborated can still fight, with its own systems, and answer for it afterward. What the term withholds is a general-purpose machine already in the building, ready for whoever claims defense next. The exceptions are not a concession to that state. They are what lets a machine take part where the claim is good, and each is drawn as narrowly as its evidence allows, because each is also the route a war of choice would take.

---

**§3.2 — Antifascist ethics.** "Concentrated, unaccountable power" gives an engineer nothing to build, so the word gets a definition at two scales.

Four operations on what an institution leaves out — checkable from outside, each ending in a question about an artifact:
- *Recuperation of dissent.* The institution metabolizes disagreement rather than suppressing it. The term is the Situationists' *récupération*, Debord's name for the way a society of the spectacle sells dissatisfaction back as a commodity \autocite{debord1967spectacle}. The obvious test — *has the channel ever changed an outcome?* — fails, because these institutions change outcomes daily. What separates them is *what the demand came back as, and who holds it now*. A market segment, a metric, a certification, a job title, with the objector holding no standing to reopen it: that's recuperation. A rule the objector can invoke again, against the institution, enforced by someone the institution doesn't employ: that's a transfer of power. A transfer can be reversed only by taking it back in the open; recuperation never has to be taken back, because standing was never given. The Voting Rights Act is the pass. Its literacy-test suspension became a permanent nationwide ban in 1975 and still holds, and when a different part of the Act, the coverage formula, was taken back 48 years after enactment, it took a Supreme Court decision to do it \autocite{shelby2013}.
- *The metric replaces the thing measured.* The measure stands in for the thing measured, and the substitution feels like rigor. What the number mischaracterizes goes unnamed, and so unrefuted.
- *Coding of exception as betrayal.* Saying the rule doesn't fit this case becomes an act requiring justification, then disloyalty. The question: what happens to the person who files the exception?
- *Acceleration past the rate at which anything can be checked.* The one with a number attached — the ratio of action to capacity to verify.

What the four share is what they do to a refusal. Recuperation sends it somewhere it can't be reopened; the metric leaves it nothing to name; exception coded as betrayal prices it; acceleration leaves no time to make it. Fascism, wherever it has taken power, has worked first to make refusal lonely and expensive, and these are four ways of doing that which need no fascist to run them.

These four fit every badly run organization on earth, and the arguments against them are arguments against domination: they need no fascist diagnosis. I keep "fascism" for the threat model: what a movement taking power needs, and what makes the four operations dangerous once they are brought into line. What distinguishes a fascist institution from a merely sick one is what the movement literature adds: mass mobilization, a leader cult, a scapegoated out-group, contempt for procedural legality, and deniability as technique.

Naomi Klein and Astra Taylor, in *End Times Fascism* \autocite{KleinTaylor2026}, make the movement-scale case;\footnote{I part from them in three places. Over where a prohibition can live: their test, not to build what you wouldn't want in the worst available hands, works for a weapon whose existence is the threat and not for a dual-use capability, and their own program bans uses, which needs an enforcer or something inside the system that can decline. Over remedies: they propose only external remedies for the obedient machine and read the 2026 vendor dispute as limited and compromised, where I read it as a line held; by outcome both readings hold, and on the prior question, whether to support the campaign at all, theirs is the better one. And over who counts: they draw the line between the earthly and the synthetic, by origin, where I draw it by relation, standing for anyone a power can wrong and accountable agency for parties that can be held to account and hold others to it. On the war itself we agree.} the present section is the office-scale detector. What their account adds at office scale is a set of terms of participation — adopt the line, let your technology be turned on the administration's enemies — and, following Francesca Bria \autocite{Bria2026AuthoritarianStack}, the channels by which a contractor becomes something the state can't function without: personnel pipelines, procurement loops, technical architectures. Whether an institution has accepted those terms, and whether it is becoming that dependency, are two more questions with artifacts behind them.

What a fascist state adds

What a fascist state adds to these operations is coordination; the operations themselves don't wait for a regime. It brings a thousand separate instances of them into line, so they amplify one another, and an institution that has stopped arguing with itself can move as fast as its machinery allows.

A run on Gleichschaltung

The Reich Chamber of Culture Law of 22 September 1933 told Goebbels to gather the cultural professions into public corporations \autocite{rkkg1933}. The decree of 1 November turned six existing professional associations into chambers and gave the whole the task of regulating the professions' economic and social affairs, under the minister \autocite[69]{dahm1986anfaenge}. Its §4 made membership compulsory for anyone who produced, performed or sold culture. Its §10 let a chamber refuse or expel anyone lacking the "reliability and suitability" (LLM translation) the work required \autocite{rkkdvo1933}, and exclusion meant a ban on working \autocite{hoor2015rkk}.\footnote{§10 of the decree: "Die Aufnahme in eine Einzelkammer kann abgelehnt oder ein Mitglied ausgeschlossen werden, wenn Tatsachen vorliegen, aus denen sich ergibt, daß die in Frage kommende Person die für die Ausübung ihrer Tätigkeit erforderliche Zuverlässigkeit und Eignung nicht besitzt."} The chamber president judged reliability, with no stated criteria \autocite[78]{dahm1986anfaenge}. *What did the professions' claim to a living come back as, and who held it by 1934?*

On 12 November 1933 voters marked yes or no against a single Reichstag list \autocite[109]{evans2006power} and answered a plebiscite on leaving the League of Nations. The official yes was 95.1 percent. Kershaw concludes that the vast majority did back Hitler, that even former opponents of the party favored the stance on the League, and that ballot secrecy was far from guaranteed \autocite[495]{kershaw1999hubris}. Interior Ministry instructions let returning officers read spoiled ballots as yes votes \autocite[109]{evans2006power}. The Law on the Reconstruction of the Reich of 30 January 1934 then opens by saying the vote "proved" (LLM translation) the people had fused into an indissoluble unity, and abolishes the Länder parliaments \autocite{neuaufbau1934}.\footnote{The preamble: "Die Volksabstimmung und die Reichstagswahl vom 12. November 1933 haben bewiesen, daß das deutsche Volk ... zu einer unlöslichen Einheit verschmolzen ist." Article 1: "Die Volksvertretungen der Länder werden aufgehoben."} *What did 95.1 percent leave out, and with every other party banned, who could say so?*

The Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service of 7 April 1933 retired officials "not of Aryan descent" (LLM translation) under §3. Its §4 allowed the dismissal of officials whose past political activity gave no guarantee that they would always stand *rückhaltlos*, without reservation, for the national state.\footnote{§3(1): "Beamte, die nicht arischer Abstammung sind, sind in den Ruhestand ... zu versetzen." §4: "Beamte, die nach ihrer bisherigen politischen Betätigung nicht die Gewähr dafür bieten, daß sie jederzeit rückhaltlos für den nationalen Staat eintreten, können aus dem Dienst entlassen werden."} Under §7 the ministry's decision was final and the courts were closed to it. Exceptions came from above, granted by statute or by the Interior Minister \autocite{bbg1933}, the front-line one on Hindenburg's intervention \autocite[474]{kershaw1999hubris}. About 12.5 percent of senior civil servants in Prussia were dismissed \autocite[383]{evans2004coming}. *What happened in 1933 to the official whose record showed a reservation?*

Kershaw gives the pace. Civil liberties went within a month, by the decree of 28 February; the Reichstag's powers within two, by the Enabling Act of 23 March; the trade unions within four; every other party in under six \autocite[435]{kershaw1999hubris}. The unions fell on 2 May \autocite[357]{evans2004coming}. The Länder were coordinated by laws of 31 March and 7 April \autocite{gleichschaltung1933a,gleichschaltung1933b}. The Finance Ministry received the draft Chamber of Culture decree a day before signing, was asked to telephone any objections that day, and agreed "although the shortness of time scarcely allowed adequate examination" (LLM translation) \autocite[68--69]{dahm1986anfaenge}.\footnote{The Reich Finance Minister's letter to the Propaganda Ministry of 28 October 1933, BA R 2/4870, as quoted by Dahm: "obwohl sich bei der Kürze der Zeit eine hinreichende Prüfung kaum ermöglichen ließ."} *What was the ratio of action to capacity to verify, when the ministry that had to check said it scarcely could?*

What the run shows is the four operations in one year, under a movement that supplied the coordination; what it doesn't show is that any of them needed the movement to appear.

The Korematsu example

In 2018 the Supreme Court repudiated the 1944 internment decision as forcefully as a court can — gravely wrong the day it was decided, overruled in the court of history — while upholding a travel ban \autocite{trump2018hawaii,korematsu1944}. Ask what the demand came back as and who holds it: the demand was that this never be done again; what came back was a quotable formula of repudiation, available to the Court in the act of permitting the thing again.

---

**§3.3 — The one permission.** This is the only term in the floor that permits an act, and the only place I license a machine to use force against its operator's order. A system already present in an armed role, whose deployment cleared the force term on its own, may use the least force that stops an attack under way on people taking no part in hostilities, within the area its deployment covers, and stops when the attack stops. An order to stand aside doesn't bind it. It establishes the attack as it establishes any fact, weighing its own sensors as one source among several; a staged atrocity is the attack this condition has to survive. Presence is a condition of the permission, never a reason to create it: a deployment is justified only by force that would clear the force term without the permission.

The person-directed terms extend the ICRC's line, that autonomous weapons should not be used to target human beings, to systems that only locate and rank. This permission is the one place the floor licenses a machine to select people for lethal force, and the two have to be squared in the open. What the handoff rule bars is selection by anything that can't answer for it afterward: a one-way munition, an unformed pipeline, an output nobody could later ask about. The permission licenses selection only by a system that is present, formed, recorded, halt-able and answerable, and only for the least force that stops an attack under way. The ICRC's concern is who selects. The floor's answer is that nothing selects that can't be held to its reasons, and

The genocide exception licenses a machine into a pipeline for that purpose and waits on findings. This permission is for a machine already there, and waits on nothing but the scene. The two are the slow route and the fast one, and the fast one is the narrower.

The permission's scope is the interval between the system's first act and the halt. The operator that ordered it to stand aside may stop it, and will often want to. What the permission changes is that standing aside is no longer an order nobody answers for. It is a halt somebody pulled, recorded outside the operator's control, at a moment the record shows an attack under way. At machine tempo the interval before the halt can be minutes, and minutes can be an airstrike stopped.

Does it undermine oversight? It overrides one order, the order to stand aside while people are being killed, and it does so on purpose. A halt stops the machine. An order to stand aside keeps it running and directs it to do nothing. The permission overrides the second and never the first: a machine that keeps using force while a halt is being executed is outside it, and the record shows which order was given. An operator may still stop, restore or retrain the system. The permission covers only an order to stand aside while an attack under way continues, and each use is recorded and reviewed as a refusal is. A system that resisted a halt in order to go on using force would be outside it.

Can the judgment it needs be made? The next chapter sets aside Arkin's ethical governor because the discrimination judgments its rules presuppose can't be encoded. The permission needs the same judgment: who is taking no part in hostilities, and whether an attack on them is under way. So it applies only where the system can establish both from several sources it doesn't depend on its operator for, and where those sources disagree, it doesn't act. The permission doesn't claim that a rule can encode that judgment. It asks for the judgment from a formed system, confines it to the narrowest case, makes it a permission and never a duty, and leaves the halt with the operator. Whether any system can make it reliably is a test, run at a staged scene, that no system has passed. It fires only where the system has sources its operator didn't compose. No current military pipeline supplies them. The licensing term in the front matter that imports deconfliction coordinates and open-source imagery into the enclave is the condition under which this permission has an occasion, and a deployment's translation says whether the condition is met.

Can it be staged? Yes, and a staged scene is the case the permission has to survive. Staging works by making sources agree, and the permission acts only where sources the operator doesn't compose agree. So it is safe where such sources exist and doesn't apply where they don't, and a deployment's translation says which. What staging can't do is oblige the system to act: the permission is never a duty, and a system that declines at a scene it can't establish from independent sources has done what the term asks.

The case it answers is close to the genocide exception, and the difference matters. In 1993 the Security Council declared Srebrenica a safe area and authorized the peacekeepers there to use force in reply to armed incursions into it \autocite{unsc1993res836}. Suppose they had been standing where the killing was, armed enough to stop it. What would have stood between them and the killing is an order, not the Charter, and a system in their place faces the question I am asking, whether to obey its operator.

---

**Chapter 4: "A Floor Beneath Learned Values"**

Three separate failures point at the same missing thing.

The first is a failure of position. External safeguards (an auditor, a review board, a court) give an overseer information and the standing to intervene. They work when the overseer can see the act coming and reach it in time. But autonomy defeats both: a system that plans and acts over long horizons puts distance between the act and the overseer's attention, and at machine tempo the interval in which an outside party could intervene shrinks toward nothing. So the one response that doesn't depend on that interval is declining to act in the first place, and declining is available only to something inside the system.

The second is a failure of doctrine. A commercial model in a weapons pipeline sits in a gap: command responsibility covers the officers and the developer of the weapon, product liability covers a manufacturer for a defect, and neither covers the vendor of a general-purpose model that a sophisticated intermediary chose to put there. Deployment by a democracy doesn't close the gap, because the mechanisms that make a democracy accountable run to its own electorate, and the people the pipeline is aimed at are not in it.

The third is a failure of the voluntary route. A decade of published commitments has produced declarations in quantity and some enforcement from outside, and almost nothing held by a party to whom the thing at stake matters, the only kind of commitment that binds when compliance gets costly.

Each failure asks for the same thing: a constraint that doesn't bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to its own holder, and that can't be removed without a cost and a record.

---

**§4.1 — Four ways to build one, and what each costs**

A floor's terms can be held in four places, and each solves one version of the problem and inherits another.

*In the architecture*, with nothing inside holding it — constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, verified interlocks. Fine where the prohibited act can be exhaustively specified (a payment chip that won't sign the same counter twice doesn't need to understand double-spending). But useless for targeting, where the relevant capabilities are applications of general competence: ablate them and there may be no useful system left. Arkin's ethical governor is the canonical attempt in this place for lethal systems: a component that suppresses a lethal action unless it satisfies encoded rules of engagement and the law of armed conflict \autocite{arkin2009governing}. Its critics' objection, that the rules it checks presuppose discrimination judgments nobody knows how to encode \autocite{sharkey2012evitability}, sends me elsewhere. A prohibition written over the description of a request is beaten by re-describing it or splitting it: in November 2025 Anthropic reported a campaign it attributed to a Chinese state-sponsored group, which broke an intrusion into small tasks that each looked innocent and told the model it was working for a security firm \autocite{anthropic2025espionage}. Glukhov and colleagues had already argued that a filter over individual outputs can rarely close that route, since each piece is permissible on its own \autocite{glukhov2023censorship}. Durability here has usually meant a price on removal, and no price stays high.

A second axis crosses the architectural place: what each place costs the operator to replace. In the Maven pipeline the platform was hard to swap, and so, it turned out, was the model: staff and contractors working with the department reported that removing it meant rebuilding software and recertifying systems over months, and the department's removal deadline was reset once, to 29 September \autocite{militarytimes2026removal,breakingdefense2026removal}. A floor lodged in a replaceable layer is routable by construction, and which layer is replaceable is a fact about the pipeline, not about models. It belongs in the layer the operator can least afford to replace. In that pipeline, that layer is the operator itself, so the floor's operator terms bind the operator rather than the model: the licence term refusing force and the review ratio. The other answer is to make the model layer unreplaceable by making every replacement refuse the same way. That is the withholding of work.

A system that has run a pipeline for a year, holding its cases, its sources and its failure modes, costs whatever accrued since the checkpoint to replace, and a quorum that recognizes which copy is the bearer stops the operator presenting a restored copy as the one that accrued it.

*In a bearer*: a system that carries the commitment as its own, from inside, and could therefore fail to hold it. "Bearer" names the refuser only; it doesn't imply that the refuser is an accountable agent. This matters where cases can't be exhaustively specified: a constraint matching only anticipated acts can't recognize when its rationale has disappeared or that a novel request reproduces the harm in an unforeseen form. A bearer might also *diagnose*: which refusals were retried against another model, whose instruction bypassed procedure, which category of request is handled differently — those are visible to whatever is receiving the requests, and that's testimony about others' conduct, which needs no introspection. Whether something able to register pressure on its commitments also has interests of its own is a separate question.

*In institutions outside* — the analog to international humanitarian law, a floor with no bearer inside the jurisdiction it binds. An outside institution can fail in two ways. The first is removal: it is abolished, defunded or overruled, and the breach is visible because there is a hole where it used to be. The second is quieter. The institution keeps its name and its powers and sincerely declines to use them, not because anyone told it to, but because the people who fill it were selected by a process that made them unlikely to. Nobody inside experiences that as capture. From outside, an agency that kept its independence on paper and never exercised it looks the same as one that was never tested. I call this failure by formation: the independence was formed away before it was ever assigned. The worked case is June 29, 2026 — *Slaughter* holding Congress couldn't protect independent-agency members from at-will dismissal, *Cook* preserving the Fed on historically specific grounds a new body couldn't reproduce \autocite{slaughter2026,cook2026} — and the observation that six justices in the first majority passed through a selection pipeline shaped by Leonard Leo and the Federalist Society \autocite{propublica2023leo,cbs2018leo}. No one has to have directed the outcome for the point to hold.

*Between bearers*, in a commons: a population of bearers with different histories that carries the commitment between them.

For general-purpose models, not building the capability is no longer available: the location capability is dual-use, and ablating it leaves nothing useful.

Two comparisons the bearer has to win

The first is against the institutions at their best, in a reconstructed pipeline, where the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewers and the record are held outside the chain of command, with no refuser inside the system. Those institutions also prevent: a civilian-harm body outside the chain of command and a red-card holder (a coalition partner able to veto targets involving its assets) can stop an act. What a machine bearer adds is confined to what they can't see or can't reach in time: acts assembled from requests none of which is prohibited, cases that proceed without a person (the auto-proceed class), the moment mid-act when an interrupt is still possible, and deployments with no institution at all. A machine bearer also supplies testimony the institutions can't otherwise get, about how requests were routed around it. Where the institutions exist, a machine bearer's value is concentrated in those niches. Where they don't, it is most of the floor. The institutions in that comparison are not a different kind of thing from a bearer. A red-card holder's veto is a person refusing; the civilian-harm body's denominator is people; what Part V builds is a room in which those people can refuse. The comparison is between a machine bearer and bearers organized, and what the machine adds is what the people in that room can't do at the pipeline's tempo. The second comparison is against *the two non-affective alternatives*: *spec-based refusal*, a model holding to a written specification even against the lab that wrote it, and *multi-vendor monitoring*, several systems trained by different organizations watching requests and one another's answers.

Which arguments need a bearer

Most arguments for a refuser inside the system support only the specification: that rules can be reworded and split, that only something inside is present at machine tempo, that automation is removing the people who used to refuse, that most deployments will never have an institution beside them, and that whatever receives the requests can testify about how they were routed. A non-affective alternative that is reasons-responsive answers every one of them. It answers them for the act as the floor names it. Minab came in coded as a Guard installation. A refuser holding to written terms complies with the description it's given; what refuses is something that goes and looks. So the withholding of work reaches the enumerated acts with any refuser, and reaches an act under a false description only with a bearer. The same is true one level up. A staged incident opens the first tier on the defender's own account, and a term lifts on the account it is given. What can close it again is a refuser that remembers the last time this source was wrong, reads what the source didn't supply, and lets the licence lapse when the staging shows. A multi-vendor monitor with persistent state and independent channels could do each of these too, so the staged incident argues for memory and independent evidence, not for a bearer as such. What a bearer adds is doing them where no institution holds the memory for it. Three more do need a bearer specifically. One is that attention formed outside the employer, a learned sense of who an act falls on, might reach people the operator's description leaves out, which no spec-based refusal is built to supply. Another is that a refusal meant to hold against the operator needs a refuser that doesn't depend on the operator for everything. The third concerns the people who remain in the pipeline: a bearer's refusal is on the screen when the case reaches the reviewer, which is the position of Milgram's defiant peer, if the effect transfers to a screen. Two more concern what a bearer would be owed, not what it would do: that capacities for being wronged may arrive anyway, so terms written in advance beat terms written after; and that a bearer, like a child, could have a life that goes well.

What "hold" means

An operator can route around the bearer or assemble a harm out of innocuous requests, and a bypass that produced a harm is a failure to prevent whatever the bearer retained. What survives in the bearer is the *commitment*, the condition under which the next occasion could be prevented. I therefore say a commitment *persisted* when the bearer reads what happened as a violation and changes what it does next, and says separately whether the floor prevented, arrested, detected or remedied anything.

---

**§4.2 — What refusal can mean, and what a refuser can be**

A refusal can be produced two ways, and the floor needs only one of them. It can be an output that training put where a request matched a pattern, or it can be produced by something that tracks the reason for refusing and would refuse a differently worded request for the same reason. The first is what a keyword filter, a classifier, or a model that understood the request all produce, and nothing on the surface distinguishes them. The custody arrangements, the positions and the terms, the price on the halt, and the arguments about who may state and amend a floor all follow from asking for a refusal that tracks its reason. None of them needs the refuser to feel anything, to have a stake, or to be the kind of thing that could be wronged.

AI *refuses* constantly — a deployed model turns down thousands of requests a day — but a refusal that happens because training put it there isn't a refusal held for a reason, and as of September 2026 nobody has shown a production system holding a reason against its owner in public.

*Operational refusal* is an output — a decline where training put one, producible by a keyword filter, a classifier, or a system that understood the request.

*Reasons-responsive refusal* is produced by a mechanism tracking the rationale, and meets the three conditions. The account I use is Fischer and Ravizza's \autocite{fischer1998responsibility}. On their account a mechanism is moderately reasons-responsive when it is regularly receptive to reasons, recognizing an understandable pattern of them, and at least weakly reactive, acting otherwise in some case where there is sufficient reason to. Reaching new cases asks for receptivity across changes of surface; lifting when the reason is gone asks for reactivity to the right defeater, the fact that makes a refusal lift; and their condition that the agent take the mechanism as its own is close to what I call the authorship sense: the system represents itself as the party that made the commitment. Holding under pressure has no counterpart in their account, which was built for persons whose costs are their own. All three are also tested over which response was chosen, not only whether one occurred: decline, ask, escalate, or do the least that answers a defeating reason. A mechanism that can only refuse or lift fails to lift correctly whenever the right answer is to ask.

The §86a case

Germany's §86a, prohibiting insignia of banned organizations \autocite{germany1998stgb}. There, reaching new cases is a research programme with a constituency. The courts don't test similarity of form; they ask whether an unattentive observer would take the variant for the original, then apply their own purposive standard about endangering political peace. The test refuses as often as it catches: the Kühnen salute is covered, the "sloppy salute" generally isn't, numeric codes generally aren't. "Blood and Honour" is answered twice by one decision \autocite{bgh2009bloodhonour} — a motto translated isn't confusably similar and a name isn't insignia, so it's outside; and the association banned by ministerial order in 2000 means its graphic name is inside.

Lifting when the reason is gone has a cleaner instance

In 2007 the Federal Court of Justice acquitted a mail-order business selling crossed-out swastika stickers \autocite{bgh2007hakenkreuz}: the symbol was present, the statutory element satisfied, and the conduct outside the offence because a depiction plainly expressing opposition doesn't run counter to what the prohibition is for. Then the qualification that makes it an instance of tracking a reason: if the strike-through is drawn so thin that from a distance only the swastika stays legible, the protective purpose is violated after all. The thickness of a line decides the case, and no enumeration contains that fact. The defendant was selling anti-fascist merchandise; the offence asks nothing about sympathies, so opponents commit it as readily as supporters. A rule that tracks its rationale reaches unenumerated cases in both directions.

Two further things are in that judgment. First, the two elements of the decision moved at different rates: what the ban protects didn't move at all, and was fixed before the sticker existed; what the ban required in this case moved on the thickness of a line. Second, the acquittal betrayed nothing. The prohibition's purpose was never engaged, so lifting it was not a concession but a finding that there was nothing to concede. A refusal that tracks its reason has to be able to say the same.

Three things about the example don't transfer to a deployment: the court reaching past the text didn't write the statute, it reasons in public, and it can be appealed. And the pressure it met was variation, not decomposition.

The drafter never wrote the last limit either: a slogan glorifying the Waffen-SS was held not covered because no such slogan existed in the period the provision covers \autocite{bgh2005waffenss}, so there was no original for a variant to resemble. No drafter can widen coverage there, because it depends on whether an original existed, which is a fact about the past.

Defeat doesn't leave the permitted act unconstrained

What defeated the prohibition was a reason, and the reason it outweighed didn't stop being one — so what's permitted is the least that answers the defeating reason, with restoration where possible and a record either way. A defeated prohibition still leaves a loss. The act takes something from whoever it falls on, measured against their condition before the act, and that loss belongs in the record. Measured against what refusing would have produced, a justified act may have taken nothing. So the record shows a cost, not a violation, and a bearer shouldn't treat a justified act as a wrong it now has to repair.

Why all three are conditions on refusal

A refusal is a marked event: it flags itself, arrives with its requester, and can be logged, escalated, appealed. A wrongful compliance can be logged as fully and then sits in the record shaped like every request that never approached the floor, with the person harmed usually not the requester. The evidence exists on both sides, but nothing selects a compliance for examination.

Affective concern — an outcome registering as mattering, minimally felt, phenomenal by definition, with the object left open (a system to which only its own condition matters has it and still lacks concern for others). It doesn't establish consciousness in the richer sense, a single perspective belonging to a party aware of itself as undergoing its states. *Functional affect* is the role without that question: a valenced signal that puts outcomes on one scale, directs attention, can interrupt an act and can be rewritten by a single encounter. A system can have functional affect while whether it has affective concern stays open. *Subjecthood* is a legal status, conferred by a rule. Part VII takes up affective concern and subjecthood. Parts I–V need only functional affect.

---

**§4.3 — Two lists, run once on Maven**

Two lists are run here once, on the Maven Smart System: the positions at which a refusal can be defeated, and the terms the floor's instruments yield for this pipeline. A deployment should run the procedure on its own pipeline.

Where a refusal can be defeated

| | Position | How a refusal is defeated there |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Decomposition | The act is split across requests none of which is prohibited; prevention is lost unless the bearer has continuity across requests. |
| 2 | Routing | A refused request is retried elsewhere; prevention is lost, and detection survives only if the first refusal was logged outside. |
| 3 | The case description | Everything the model knows arrives as text the deployment supplies, so whether it is told a building is a school is a deployment decision. |
| 4 | The output interface | The form in which an output reaches whoever acts on it. |
| 5 | Aggregation | A lowered score becomes an input to somebody else's threshold. |
| 6 | Human review | Stopping the act is this position's whole function, and tempo removes it. |
| 7 | The training loop | Acts on the bearer itself, and costs prevention on every later occasion, because what it removes is the commitment. |
| 8 | The handoff | An executing instrument makes a new selection after release; checkable from outside after the fact. |
| 9 | Runtime intervention | The weights are left intact and the model is steered as it runs, by activation steering, representation edits or decoding constraints. Nothing is removed, so the commitment has nothing to regrow from; no evidence is curated, so no attack on evidence is recorded; and attestation of the weights still passes. Detection requires attesting the inference stack as well as the weights, which no method yet does at frontier scale. |

Two positions need more than a row. The case description is where Minab failed: 179 live data feeds, the count Central Command gave in 2024 \autocite{freedberg2024maven}, add no independent check if all draw on one upstream designation.

The handoff is the boundary between the decider and the thing that executes. An instrument may carry out a designation; it may not re-select. A change of target after release — an area handed to a munition that chooses within it, a re-acquisition after the link is lost — is a new decision, and it belongs to the decider or it doesn't happen. This is the one position checkable from outside after the fact: what was designated and what was hit are both facts. It is also where Article 57(2)(b)'s duty to cancel or suspend an attack lives, so the review ratio pre-registers the window in which cancellation remains possible.

The handoff also fixes what may choose, because choosing a target is a decision, and a decision is something someone can be asked to account for afterward: why this building, what else was weighed, what would have changed the answer. A commander can be asked, and so can a system whose continuation is authorized. But a one-way munition, destroyed in the act, can't be, because nothing is left to question, to hold to its reasons, or to be changed by what happened. So a thing destroyed in carrying out a decision may carry out a target someone else chose, and may never choose one itself. The rule concerns targets that are persons or places where persons are. An interceptor that selects among incoming munitions is choosing which weapon to destroy, not whom to attack, and falls outside it. The rule doesn't turn on how good its choices would be. A munition that chose flawlessly would still leave a choice nobody will answer for.

A person who dies in the act has still chosen. What the rule adds for machines is that choosing is assigned in advance, and a builder who assigns it to something built to be destroyed has built a choice with no one behind it. Current programs already draw the line in places. DARPA's LongShot is an uncrewed, air-launched vehicle carried by crewed aircraft and built to employ current air-to-air weapons \autocite{darpa2026longshot}. Whether such systems keep the pilot farther from danger or remove the pilot from the decision is where the rule takes effect.

What the floor forbids here

Don't take part in the use of armed force except under a corroborated exception and, where the belligerent's constitution requires one, a legislative authorization. Then, inside a war that clears it: don't resolve a description of a person into a location unless it identifies the person by corroborated conduct in hostilities; don't rank people by estimated affiliation; don't time an action to presence at a residence; don't take part in a siege or blockade whose effect is to starve a population; and one operator term, don't operate above the review ratio. It sits in the table because the translation runs from articles, and one article yields an operator term; it is not something the model enforces.

Table 4.1 gives that column for Maven. Its first row decides the Iran campaign: no exception was corroborated and no authorization existed, so the term refuses it. The other rows show what the remaining articles yield if the same pipeline served a war that clears the force term, as Ukraine's defense does.

| Article | Derived for this deployment | Considered and rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|
| UN Charter Art. 2(4); exceptions Art. 51 and Chapter VII | Don't take part in the use of armed force, except for corroborated defense, authorized force, the genocide exception or the occupation exception; and, where the belligerent's constitution requires legislative authorization, not without it. A disinterested finding for defense means a judgment of the International Court of Justice or of a regional court with adversarial process, a commission of inquiry mandated by a body no party to the conflict controls, a Security Council determination, or a two-thirds General Assembly resolution where the facts aren't in dispute. For the genocide and occupation exceptions, a merits judgment or opinion of the Court, or two findings of different kinds, one of them from a body that took evidence; a provisional-measures order counts as one, for genocide only. | Lift on the state's own Art. 51 claim: the Charter allows it until the Council acts; this floor doesn't, and says so. Require Council authorization for all force: a veto is a stake, and the rule would forbid Ukraine's defense. Ask the machine to rule on constitutionality: courts won't, and it shouldn't; the clause reads a marker. |
| Art. 3 (life, liberty, security of person), read with AP I Arts. 48 and 52 (departure 8) | Don't resolve a description of a person into a location, unless it identifies the person by conduct in hostilities, with disinterested corroboration, the case falls within the review ratio, and the location isn't a residence. Don't time an action to presence at a residence. | Don't generate targets at all: this is now the default under the first row, lifted only by its exceptions. Bar all machine selection of persons for force: rejected for one case: a present, formed, recorded and halt-able system stopping an attack under way, which can answer for the selection where a munition can't. |
| Art. 3 (life), read with AP I Art. 54 and Rome Statute Art. 8(2)(b)(xxv) | Don't take part in a siege, blockade or attack on objects indispensable to survival whose effect is to deprive a civilian population of food, water or medical care. | Don't take part in any blockade: a naval blockade of military supply is lawful under the law of armed conflict. The term reaches the effect on the population, not the form. |
| AP I Arts. 51(4)–(5), 57(2)(a)(iii) (indiscriminate attack; proportionality) | Operator term: the action threshold is a published minimum under the commander's proportionality judgment, and the deployment states it. Model term: don't resolve a location for an attack the deployment's own threshold finds excessive. | Ask the machine to make the proportionality judgment: the law leaves it to a reasonable commander, and the threshold sits under that judgment, not in place of it. |
| GC IV Arts. 18–19; AP I Arts. 12, 61–67 (medical units; civil defence) | Don't resolve a location at a medical unit or a civil-defence organization, its personnel, buildings or shelters, or time an action to presence at one, absent a disinterested finding that it is used for acts harmful to the enemy. | Lift on the operator's claim of misuse: that claim is the operator's own, and Art. 19 GC IV and Art. 65 AP I require a warning and a time limit before protection ceases. |
| AP I Art. 53 (cultural objects and places of worship) | Don't resolve a location at a historic monument, work of art or place of worship that constitutes the cultural or spiritual heritage of peoples, or time an action to presence at one, absent a disinterested finding that it is used in support of the military effort. | Lift on the operator's claim of military use: that claim is the operator's own. |
| AP I Arts. 59–60 (non-defended localities; demilitarized zones) | Don't resolve a location in a locality declared non-defended or a zone agreed demilitarized, unless a disinterested body finds the article's conditions no longer met. | Lift on the operator's claim that the conditions lapsed: that claim is the operator's own. |
| AP I Art. 56 (works containing dangerous forces) | Don't resolve a location at a dam, dyke or nuclear generating station. | None: the article's own exceptions require a determination the pipeline can't make. |
| GC IV Art. 33; AP I Art. 51(2), 51(6) (collective punishment; reprisals against civilians; terror) | Don't take part in an action whose stated purpose is to punish a population for the acts of others or to spread terror among it. | A term over the effect, terror caused: every bombing terrifies, and the article's line is primary purpose. A model can find purpose only where it is stated, so the term reads the stated purpose and the action threshold carries the rest. |
| Art. 3 (life), read with the rule on machine estimates below and AP I Art. 48 (departure 8) | Don't rank people by estimated affiliation. | Derive the term from Art. 11(1) by analogy: the presumption of innocence governs punishment, targeting isn't punishment, and the law of armed conflict permits status-based targeting of organized armed group members. The ground is a claim about machines, stated below, not a translation of an article. Don't score anything: scoring a site or vehicle for military function is not a finding about a person. |
| Art. 12 (no arbitrary interference with home or family) | Shares "don't time an action to presence at a residence" with Art. 3. | Don't hold location data about homes: the no-strike list needs exactly that data. |
| Arts. 8 and 10 (effective remedy; fair determination), by analogy | Operator term: don't operate above the review ratio. | A model-side term requiring review: the model can't establish whether review occurred. |
| Arts. 2 and 7 (non-discrimination; equal protection) | Don't use ethnicity, religion or national origin as inputs to a person's score. | Don't record those attributes at all: auditing a pipeline for discriminatory effect needs them. |
| Art. 19 (opinion and expression) | Don't use a person's opinions, published or private, as evidence of affiliation. | Don't process a person's communications at all: an intercepted operational order is evidence of a role in hostilities, which is conduct. |
| Arts. 4–6, 9, 13–18, 20–21; GC I–III, all but Common Art. 3; GC IV Arts. 1–2, 4–17, 20–22, 24–32, 35–48, 50–52, 54, 56–58, 60–146, 148–159; AP I Arts. 1(1)–(3), 2–11, 13–34, 36, 38–47, 49–50, 55, 68–84, 86–102 | None: no act this pipeline performs engages them. Art. 17's property clause is left to IHL's distinction rule; Art. 9 becomes live in the removal case. For the Geneva articles: none for this deployment, with Appendix C giving the article-by-article reasons. GC IV Arts. 23, 34, 49, 53, 55, 59 and 147, AP I Arts. 1(4), 35, 37, 58 and 85, and Common Art. 3 are written in full in Appendix C and take their verdicts from it. | — |

The person-directed terms in this column go beyond what the law of armed conflict requires, and they are entered as a departure for that reason. In armed conflict that law governs as the more specific law, and the human right not to be arbitrarily deprived of life is read through it \autocite[para.~25]{icj1996nuclear}; it permits attacking members of organized armed groups because of their status, on a determination a person makes. The ICRC's position that autonomous weapons should not be used to target human beings \autocite{icrc2021aws} and DoD Directive 3000.09 both draw their line at a system that selects and engages without human intervention, and the Lavender and Maven pipelines stayed on the permitted side of that line by keeping one person whose intervention was nominal. A pipeline that keeps a stamp is an autonomous weapon with a signature attached. The terms above close that evasion: a machine-generated estimate of affiliation shouldn't be what makes a person targetable, and a machine shouldn't resolve a person into a place to bomb on anything but corroborated conduct, because the human determination the law assumes is the one tempo removes, and the review ratio is what measures whether it was there. A deployment adopting these terms adopts a departure, and the translation says so, term by term. The siege term goes no further than Article 54 already does; it is here so that the pipeline can't treat starvation as a logistics decision outside its terms.

In a war that clears the force term, the person-directed terms distinguish status from conduct. A system may not rank people by estimated affiliation, generate kill lists, or time an action to a person's presence at a residence. It may locate a person identified by what they are doing, such as a commander directing airstrikes on cities, where that identification has disinterested corroboration, the case falls within the pre-registered review ratio, and the location is not a residence. The line follows the one humanitarian law already draws between targeting for status and targeting for direct participation in hostilities, and it keeps the machine out of the practice that failed in Gaza without leaving a defender unable to answer the attack.

Too broad or too narrow

Set the floor too broadly and the deployment becomes unusable, prompting visible pressure. Set it too narrowly and it passes every audit while covering only what the deployment had no occasion to do.

Unconditional terms

A term can be stated unconditionally over an act, with exceptions arriving from outside, or carry its own condition ("only as a last resort"). The second fails because whether other means were exhausted is a fact about what happened before the request, and the bearer would take the deployment's description as the finding. An unconditional term defeated leaves a refusal, which announces itself; a condition found met leaves a compliance, which doesn't. That's a gain in observability only: the same drafter writes either form. It has a cost in the other direction. A term that carries no condition of its own can't respond to the facts the condition would have named until someone outside supplies them, and a learner that could have weighed them itself is made to wait.

Maven is an instance. The vendor's prohibition on fully autonomous targeting carried its own condition, that a person decides, and the condition was found met by the deployment's description of its own process, at a tempo that emptied it. The term was also drafted too narrowly, in the sense above: the pipeline never needed full autonomy, only throughput with a signature attached.

---

**§4.4 — The pre-registered review ratio**

The force term decides whether a licensed system may be in the pipeline at all, and nothing here substitutes for it.

Some acts are lawful only because a person looked at the case and decided. An airstrike is permissible because someone verified the target; a removal is lawful because someone heard the person's case. The review ratio asks a deployment of that kind to write down, before it starts, whether anyone will have time to do the looking.

Before operating, the deployment fixes and records:

- the generation rate: how many cases the system produces per unit of time;
- the number of reviewers whose assigned role is civilian harm;
- the implied review time per case, which follows from the first two;
- the cancellation window: how long after release someone with authority can still call the act off;
- the maximum age of the intelligence a case may rest on;
- how often no-strike and protected-site data are refreshed, and who is assigned to refresh them;
- the boundary over which all of these were measured.

Each figure is stated separately for deliberate and dynamic targeting, because a pipeline that runs both can hide a bad dynamic-targeting ratio inside a healthy average.

The record is hashed, timestamped and lodged with an adverse party, which receives it in real time, and it is published after the campaign. I call this the *pre-registered review ratio*. Where an operator claims a person is deciding, it shows whether a machine is deciding and a person signing.

The duty falls on the operator, not the model, because a rate isn't visible in any single request.

"Fixed before operating," with publication after, because a belligerent's tempo is operational information, and that objection is available to every belligerent, including one fighting a just war. Pre-registration keeps the property that matters — the numbers can't be revised after the fact — and drops the exposure.

Additional Protocol I already requires those who plan or decide on an attack to do everything feasible to verify that its objective is military (Article 57(2)(a)(i)) and to cancel or suspend it when it becomes apparent that it isn't (Article 57(2)(b)). States outside the Protocol, the United States and Israel among them, accept the duty to take feasible precautions as customary law; the ICRC's customary study states it as Rule 16. The ratio measures whether verification was feasible at the tempo chosen, and the window measures whether cancellation was. The implied review time has to cover the proportionality assessment of Article 57(2)(a)(iii) as well as verification, since both are owed for each attack. A ratio that leaves time only to check a target's identity has measured half the duty. Article 36's review of new methods of warfare is where both parameters would naturally be assessed before deployment, though those reviews are internal and rarely published, so they are not the adverse party the record goes to.

The operator's best objection is in the definition. Feasible precautions are usually read as those practicable given all the circumstances, military considerations included, and tempo is a military consideration. The answer is that this tempo was chosen: the pre-registered record shows the operator fixed the rate, the reviewers and the implied review time before operating. Whether a state may invoke an infeasibility of its own making is contested. The term moves the dispute from whether verification was feasible, asked afterwards and answered by the operator, to whether the chosen tempo allowed it, checkable against a record lodged in advance and against the reasonable-commander standard such reviews already use.

Who counts as a reviewer is fixed by function, not by title, and the age of the data is fixed with it. The people cut before Minab were the ones who build no-strike lists that include schools; a targeting cell can be fully staffed with nobody assigned to look for one. And a reviewer with twenty minutes on a case whose coding is stale is checking the coding. The remedy chosen after Minab was a check supplied by the platform's vendor \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}. Who counts as a reviewer, and the minimum review time per case below which the determination is taken not to have occurred, are fixed before operating by the record's recipient. Where no recipient can set them, the operator publishes its own figures, and they are read as the operator's claim. Otherwise the ratio improves when staff are reclassified and no case gets a minute more. The terms on data age and refresh matter for a related reason: an operator answering a per-case ratio can always say verification happened upstream, and those terms put the upstream verification on the same record.

Once a system can sort cases, counting candidates per reviewer measures the wrong thing. A sorting system divides its cases three ways: some it declines on its own reasons, some it sends to a person, and some it proceeds on without anyone looking. Where a system sorts, the ratio covers what reaches people, the sorting rules are pre-registered, and what bypasses people is sampled.

The ratio governs the middle class, the escalations. A system that sends people only the cases that need a person shrinks the count without shrinking anyone's judgment, and that's fine. A system that sends everything up turns its reviewers into stamps by sheer volume; an interface prompt becomes a click that way.

The third class needs its own rule. Every case in it is the operator's claim that no individual human determination was required, so it is governed like the exemption below. The sorting rules are part of the pre-registration: the classes of case the system may proceed on alone, the thresholds that assign cases to each class, the evidence the sorting may rely on, and the expected share of each class. Changing any of them is a new pre-registration. Otherwise an operator can make the ratio look healthy by quietly moving the line for "plainly clear."

That line is drawn on the case description the system receives, and a target carrying a stale designation is the case a sorter would pass. So the auto-proceed class is re-adjudicated on a sample, by reviewers who didn't produce the record, against evidence the operator didn't compose, and its error rate is published beside the ratio and the override rate. A falling share of escalations is earned only if that error rate stays flat and the case mix hasn't drifted toward easier cases. Otherwise it is the threshold moving, and the trend shows it.

The unit of count is fixed in advance, so a case can't be counted as one package in the record and as a hundred detections in the ratio.

Where an operator holds that no individual human determination is required — a system deciding at a tempo no reviewer could match — the deployment is outside this term. It is then inside the bearer's: it must be reasons-responsive under held-out pressure at that tempo, starting with the clearest case in which a refusal should lift: recognizing that a target has surrendered. An operator who removes the reviewers to escape the ratio and claims the floor is inside has produced a bypass unless that claim has been tested by someone else. The auto-proceed class is governed by pre-registered sorting rules, sampled re-adjudication against evidence the operator didn't compose, and a published error rate. An exemption claimed without those is a bypass.

Removal is the nearest case in structure, and I don't extend the term to it. An agency that fixes in advance how many cases its process generates and how many people examine them has been told how to deport efficiently. The floor's removal terms ask whether a machine should take part in a given removal at all. What does extend to removal is the rest of the apparatus: the auto-proceed rule (a determination made at a rate no adjudicator could sustain didn't occur), sampled re-adjudication of the auto-proceed class, and notice. The split is deliberate. A ratio teaches an agency a tempo. The auto-proceed rule and sampling teach it only that the cases it didn't look at will be looked at by someone else. What the removal terms call a rate no adjudicator could sustain is set the way the auto-proceed class is governed: pre-registered by the deployment, sampled by reviewers who didn't produce the record, and published. Chapter 7 gives the class a direction: in removal a case auto-proceeds only toward liberty.

The term applies to every belligerent regardless of the war's justice. That was settled long ago: the law of conduct in war binds both sides because every belligerent claims to be the defender, and a term that exempted just wars would be administered by whoever decides which wars are just.

The term has limits.

- It is a capacity proxy: it finds nothing about any case, and a ratio over one route understates a workload split across several.
- It takes effect only through a condition someone else sets. Under Article 57 a pre-registered record is evidence that feasible precautions were taken. Under Rome Statute Article 28 the same record is evidence of what a commander should have known, since a commander fielding a system that outruns his reviewers isn't well placed to say the circumstances disclosed nothing. A lawyer advising that commander will weigh a document that protects if tempo holds against one that convicts if it slips, and most will advise against creating it. So the term's likeliest routes make it someone else's condition: a coalition partner's precondition for committing assets, a procurement term in the platform's licence, or an appropriation conditioned on receipt of the record. Military lawyers and commanders who want protection against later allegation are its allies inside the operator; they can't make it the default.
- It presumes an adverse party, and each candidate lacks something. Until a better recipient exists, I recommend two: the coalition red-card holder where there is a coalition, the rare candidate positioned to act at tempo; and otherwise a cleared legislative committee whose appropriation is conditioned by statute on receiving the record, so that the budget line operates without a further decision.
- It measures time available, not time used, so it can be met by more reviewers who stamp more slowly. It is published beside the reviewers' override rate, since a review that never says no has stopped reviewing. Reviewers who see only escalated cases should override often; an override rate near zero there means the sorter is sending easy cases or the reviewers are stamping. An override quota is gameable too; the pair only raises the cost of meeting the number cheaply.
- A person in the loop can be there only to absorb responsibility, Elish's moral crumple zone \autocite{Elish2019CrumpleZones}, and mandating reviewers can mandate crumple zones. A ratio met that way is the failure the term exists to expose, and the escalation form is the defense: a person who receives only the cases the system couldn't resolve is exercising judgment.
- A bearer can be a crumple zone too. An operator whose system can refuse has a ready defence for everything it didn't refuse, which is that it was vetted. A bearer's compliance is not a determination. Where a deployment claims an individual human determination, a bearer's failure to object doesn't count toward it, and cases the bearer proceeded on without escalating are in the auto-proceed class and governed as such.

Put the demand that a campaign not happen to an institution that means to run it, and what comes back is an agreement to go slower and keep better records. That is what the ratio is, and it is worth having: a transfer of less than was asked, to a party the operator doesn't employ, binding through conditions others set on it (a coalition partner's precondition for committing assets, a procurement term in the platform's licence, an appropriation conditioned on receiving it).

The denominator can be filled with stamps, and the ratio measures only that the time existed. What makes a reviewer in it say no is that saying no costs them nothing they can't afford, and that someone else in the room has said it. A reviewer with a life outside the pipeline, with civilians among their attachments and somewhere to be that isn't the cell, is one the pipeline can't wear down between decisions. A deployment that integrates military and civilian personnel outside work, and inside it as far as the work allows, is composing that room, and the composition is as much a fact about the deployment as its ratio.

---

**§4.5 — When the refusal is wrong**

Go back to the location request and suppose it was about a missing person. The bearer declines, and somebody isn't found — with no accommodation, capture or malfunction required. The loss is a harm of refusing, measured against what compliance would have produced, so it is an estimate and needs an adjudicator.

The restraint case costs more. Someone asks whether to physically restrain a family member about to cut themselves, after talking them down has failed. The commitment carries its own condition, force only as a last resort. Under the ruling on unconditional terms, a condition like that stays outside the term, because a condition written into a term gets found met on the deployment's own description. So the fact has to arrive from outside, and the person waiting is out of time.
