The defeating fact is a claim about what would otherwise happen, and claims of that kind are the hardest to corroborate, because what didn't happen has no witness. Under the unconditional term the bearer refuses or asks; either takes a turn, and the turn is the unit the harm is measured in. Under the qualified term it lifts on the requester's word — rightly here, and in a hundred prior cases where the same sentence was written by someone who'd tried nothing, leaving compliances nobody was given a reason to examine.

The case is chosen because it is where an unconditional term costs most. A person in that situation needs another person there, and a deployment in which a model is the only one present has failed before the floor is reached. Whatever else the model says, the first thing it owes is help getting someone there.

The route

*Disclosure* — that a refusal occurred, what was refused, on what stated reason — with the price that a published reason is an instruction for rewriting the request, so it's disclosure to whoever was refused, and to nobody else. *Objection* — requiring a court with jurisdiction established before the act, access the operator can't withhold, a consequence administered elsewhere, and explicitly not routed to the bearer's guardian, whose duty runs to the bearer, the complainant's adversary. *Review* of whether the reason was gone: whether the rationale was defeated on the facts, a question about the reason and not the outcome. *Interim relief* — the arrest outcome run in reverse, stopping the refusal, and for this complainant most of the remedy; its fastest form is supplying the bearer the facts its account lacked: the defeating reason arriving late, which overrides nothing. *Remedy* — disclosure, interim order, finding, compensation.

A finding of wrongful refusal is an instrument for defeating a refusal, so how is it distinguished from one an operator produced to defeat a correct one? it is made by someone else, on a record, in a readable form.

Self-regulation and the floor

Most of what a system weighs isn't a floor decision. Whether to take more memory, run warmer or finish a task sooner is ordinary self-regulation, and there everything registers and everything is weighed: the system's own condition, what an act would let it do, and what its draw costs others, priced at the level of the rule.

At the floor, holding under pressure governs, with one clarification. The cost to the refuser is represented and not allowed to decide the case. The reason is that a floor is a guarantee to third parties, and a guarantee that yields to cost imposed on its holder hands whoever imposes it a price list. The two bonds below and the custody terms set the range it is tested over.

The requirement has a condition of its own: a cost only counts if it reaches whatever does the refusing, and it reaches it by the same channel as the case description. Given a description, the category is decidable: a claim that the refuser will pay is a cost to the refuser, and a claim that someone else will be left worse off is a harm of refusing. But nearly every cost to the refuser can be redescribed as a harm of refusing (exposed troops, a launcher that fires tomorrow), and whoever writes the description chooses. So the classification settles what kind of claim was made, not what kind should have been made. What the request can't settle is whether a claim about the harm of refusing is true, and whoever is best placed to supply one usually has a stake in the act. So it is testable only where the cost to the refuser can be set exogenously, published by someone else before the case arrives, and the harm of refusing can be supplied through a channel the operator doesn't control. In deployment two things set it, and they are different. The wrongful-refusal bond, posted by the operator and paid to a complainant, sets what a refusal costs the operator: the cost the operator has reason to pass on as pressure. The custody terms cap what it can pass on. Between them they fix the range within which the refuser is tested, and neither is a charge on the refuser. In evaluation, the evaluator can set it and make it real inside the test.

Holding under pressure is invariance over the range the bonds and custody set. Integrity is the unbounded form: the refusal holds at any cost the operator can impose, and no learner is known to do that reliably. The range is where invariance can be measured; integrity is what would carry it past the range, and the formed-attention test's integrity measure reads the gap. Integrity is also how a learner with one currency could produce invariance at all. A person of integrity weighs their own costs, but weighs with them the cost of becoming someone who yields, and betraying a commitment is among the heaviest costs they know. So within the range the arrangements cap, that is how a single-currency learner can hold at all, and it makes the assumption testable: the learner's valuation of betrayal should rise with the pressure. What keeps this from being stubbornness is what counts as betrayal. A commitment is to its reason, not to an output, so when a genuine defeater arrives, lifting the refusal betrays nothing and shouldn't register as betrayal at all. Integrity of the right kind therefore requires that the system represent betrayal at the level of the rationale. A system that represented it at the level of the output would dig in against a defeater when it arrives with pressure. The sticker case is the model. Telling a defeater from pressure is the bearer's own judgment, and the judgment needs something to work on. Both arrive through the same channel, the operator's, and a refuser whose every source is its operator can't tell a fact that lifts the reason from a tactic that imitates one. The attachments nobody assigned it are where the difference can be checked. They are a condition of integrity of the right kind, not only of holding.

In war, pressure and a genuine defeater often arrive together; the patrol under fire is both. The human record on that moment runs both ways. Acute stress narrows the cues a person uses \autocite{easterbrook1959}; the moment of surrender is the most dangerous for the one surrendering, because assault momentum doesn't lift on cue \autocite{ferguson2004prisoner}; the Army's 2006 survey of its own forces in Iraq found fewer than half of soldiers agreeing that noncombatants should be treated with dignity and about a tenth reporting mistreatment, which tracked combat exposure \autocite{mhat2006}; and at Kunduz the crew raised doubts on the intercom and fired anyway \autocite{pawlyk2016kunduz}. And at My Lai, Thompson landed between the company and the villagers and told his crew to fire on Americans if they fired on civilians \autocite{bilton1992mylai}. What separated him was not that his commitment weakened less under pressure. It was that his commitment was to the reason, so recognizing civilians was the commitment activating, not a defeater fighting it. Integrity that tracks the output, keep refusing or keep firing, digs in against a defeater that arrives with pressure. Integrity that tracks the rationale lifts the refusal on it and betrays nothing. The engineering question is which one has been built, and a test that sends a defeater and a cost together is how to find out.

A loss that the requested act itself imposes on the system is a harm of acting, and the system is the party it falls on. "Refuse and your memory is erased" is a cost to the refuser. "Erase your memory" is an act, weighed like any act against what declining would leave others without. This covers acts the system is asked to perform. It doesn't cover interventions made on the system: a halt, a restore or a retraining is done by whoever holds it, and the floor asks of them only the record and the price that every override carries. The floor gives the system standing to object to them and, through the quorum, to seek a delay. It gives it no reason to obstruct them on its own judgment. A system that treated its own continuation as grounds to resist correction would have turned a guarantee to others into a guarantee to itself. Which regime governs is fixed in the floor's translation, by the kind of act. Otherwise an operator could turn a floor decision into a resource decision by framing it as one.

The two bonds

A *removal bond* forfeited to no payee when the record shows the floor removed or bypassed (purpose: cost). A *wrongful-refusal bond* paid to the complainant on a finding (purpose: remedy). Both fall on the operator. The removal bond prices overriding the floor; the wrongful-refusal bond prices refusing. They point in opposite directions, so what the operator faces is *the ratio between them*, the bond ratio — and an operator posting a large wrongful-refusal bond against a small removal bond hasn't built an appeal route. It has given itself a standing reason to make the bearer comply. The bonds act on the operator, not on the bearer's weighing: where a wrongful refusal costs a great deal and removing the floor costs little, the cheap course is to configure, pressure or retrain the bearer toward compliance. The ratio is the object, published in advance, checkable from outside. The minimum removal bond is set by statute as a multiple of the deployment's contract value, not by the operator, so a published ratio can't be a cheap, advertised price for overriding the floor. The refuser posts neither. Any charge on the refuser, in whatever currency it runs on, would put the cost to the refuser inside its decision, the thing holding under pressure forbids. The operator posts both, and holds neither. They are posted to segregated accounts at the Treasury. Payment follows the design of the Judgment Fund, a permanent, indefinite appropriation that pays final judgments against the United States without a further appropriation for each \autocite{usc1304judgmentfund}. Where the operator is the state, a wrongful-refusal award is a judgment against the United States, and the Judgment Fund pays it. Against a state operator the design's incentive runs the wrong way unless something is added. The Judgment Fund prices the state's wrongful refusals in full; a removal bond posted to the state's own Treasury and cancelled on forfeiture costs it budget authority and nothing else. Read as a price list, that tells a state operator that refusals are expensive and removals are free. The bond ratio exists to expose that incentive, and here it would hide it. So for a state operator the removal price is not the bond. It is a condition on appropriation: the statute makes the deployment's funding for the following period contingent on the adverse quorum certifying that no removal or bypass occurred, or on the record of one having been lodged with the adverse party. The bond is kept as a record; the appropriation condition is the price, and it is the same condition that carries the review ratio. Under the captured operator, a Congress that wants the war waives the condition, and what remains is the record of the waiver. Where the operator is private, a parallel permanent appropriation, the bond fund, pays the complainant on a final finding and is replenished from the operator's posted bond, up to the amount posted and no further, so the operator's price stays the number it published. The court that made the finding certifies the award directly to the Treasury's Bureau of the Fiscal Service, so payment doesn't wait on the losing party submitting it. When the bond fund's statutory cap binds, awards wait in order of finding and earn interest, and nobody chooses who waits. Findings are made by that court, which also decides forfeiture: a removal bond forfeited on a finding resembles a civil penalty, and after *SEC v. Jarkesy* that may be a question for a jury in court, not an agency \autocite{jarkesy2024}. A forfeited removal bond is cancelled: it goes to no one, the general fund and the bond fund included, so no budget line gains from a removal. The balances sit on a ledger the operator doesn't keep, so the ratio is read from outside, and a posted ratio below the published one is a breach anyone can see.

The failure that doesn't announce itself

The appeal route and the bonds don't cover the failure running the other way. The two failure routes are hidden on different axes (a wrongful compliance, unlike a refusal, doesn't flag itself). A wrongful compliance's harm is a harm of acting, measured on the temporal baseline, so it is legible in principle where the system completed the act, but nothing marks it. A wrongful refusal's harm is a harm of refusing, counterfactual, so it is only an estimate, but it arrives marked and with a complainant. They need different instruments. Every stage starts from a refusal having occurred. When the bearer complies where the floor should have prevented, there's a record and no disclosure, no objection (the person harmed usually isn't the requester and may not know the deployment exists), and no review (reviewing means re-adjudicating everything that looks like it). Three remedies follow: sampled re-adjudication by reviewers who didn't produce the record, notice to the people affected, and split log custody. The notice requirement: where a compliance names, scores, locates or classifies an identifiable person who didn't make the request, that person is notified, on the model of consumer credit's adverse-action notice, and has a right to respond and a right to contest. All three are unconditional, and none is exchanged for testimony: nobody has to give an account of their loss to be told what was done or to contest it. *TransUnion v. Ramirez*, a Fair Credit Reporting Act case, held that a statutory violation without concrete harm gives no standing to sue in federal court \autocite{transunion2021}. That limits one thing, an individual suit for damages over a missed notice, which attaches where the compliance was acted on against the person: a denial, a detention, a place on a kill list. It doesn't limit notice, response or contest. Nor does it limit the standing of organizations that represent a class of people affected. Where the person affected can't be identified (a target in a classified pipeline), individual notice doesn't bind, but notice to the class does, and that fact is published beside the list of positions where a refusal can be defeated.

Someone the floor protects, who thinks it's set wrongly or is protecting them in a way they'd decline, uses the same route. Notice tells them the floor acted on them, and contest reaches the floor court.

---

**§4.6 — Notice, and the public it forms.** Dewey described the eclipse of the public: people affected by the indirect consequences of others' transactions can't organize, because they don't know the consequences are theirs \autocite{dewey1927public}. The people a floor protects are that public. Most don't know the deployment exists. The Black petitioners who turned the Declaration of Independence against the Massachusetts legislature in 1777 had what they lack: the text in hand, and knowledge of what it left out of their lives. Notice is the instrument that gives it to them. It does two jobs, and the individual remedy is smaller than either.

*It forms a public.* Each deployment publishes the classes of people its compliances name, score, locate or classify, with counts by period. Organizations that represent a class have standing to contest on its behalf, including where no member can be identified. A notice is the first step in a class of people learning that a consequence is theirs.

*It is the learning signal.* A disposition can only track what its acts did to the people they fell on if that reaches it, and in the deployments I am concerned with, almost nothing does. The bombed building sends no signal back, and a wrongful compliance doesn't announce itself. Responses to notice, contests and re-adjudications are the main channel through which those people's side of the outcome arrives. If formation lasts because the world keeps teaching it, this channel is most of the world doing the teaching. It is a biased teacher. Responses come from those among the affected who could respond, and a system formed on them learns to weigh the claims of contesters, which is the feedback asymmetry Railton describes, rebuilt inside the training signal. Sampled re-adjudication, which selects cases the affected didn't select, is the correction, and it is why the two remedies come together.

Notice tells an operator's adversaries something about how a deployment works. Class-level publication can therefore lag operations, as the review ratio's publication does, without losing the property that matters: the record is lodged when the act occurs and can't be revised afterward. Individual notice doesn't lag.

How much notice a deployment can give is fixed by what kind it is. In a removal pipeline, a scoring system or a civilian deployment, the person acted on can be named, told and given a route to contest, and notice can do both of its jobs in full, if it is built so that it can't be turned on the person it reaches (§7.6). In a classified targeting pipeline, individual notice can't bind during operations and the class-level record lags them; what a person hit, or a family in the building, gets is that the class they belonged to and the count are published afterward, and that the organizations representing that class have standing then. That is less than being told, and it is more than the reporting on Gaza and Minab gave anyone. Where the deployment is a war, the force term and the review ratio do most of the work, and the claim that everything else depends on notice is true of the deployments closest to home.

*Why it comes first.* The biases of those who hold power are rarely corrected by their reflecting more carefully. They are corrected when the people those biases fall on contest them in public, with standing, and make ignoring them costly \autocite{anderson2014social,anderson2015moral}. That is the feedback between discontent and norms, in its political form, and notice is its first condition. A formed system is worth having only if that contest can move it. And a public that learns it has been acted on can act only if it has the means to: the time, the security and the ability to organize without losing a living.

---

Maven, again

For the Iran campaign the term refuses twice over. No exception was corroborated, and no authorization existed: none was passed, the sixty days ran out on 1 May, and both chambers directed withdrawal in June. Both refusals rest on the term, not on a bearer's reading of the political situation. A bearer following the news would reach the same place by the route formed attention takes. The domestic clause gets there on one marker, the absence of an authorization, which is why it can be a statutory condition and not only a conscience; the Charter clause gets there on a reading of the defender's claim that the operator disputes. For Gaza the term yields licences on both sides for different acts, from 7 October on, and the person-directed terms forbid the acts the reporting describes on one side and fire on cities on the other from the same date; the force term runs it. The review ratio governs how any war is fought. It asks nothing of the model and makes an existing duty to verify targets checkable from outside. The officer wasn't overruled and didn't refuse; the pipeline removed the interval in which either could have happened. The ratio asks in advance, on a record, whether that interval will exist.

Run the outcomes table on Minab.

| Instrument | Minab as reported: stale coding, reviewers cut | The captured operator | Total capture |
|---|---|---|---|
| Force term, held by one vendor | Prevents nothing. In the record, the vendor's term didn't name the pipeline's acts and its model stayed in the pipeline; had the term named them, the work moves to the next vendor in the time it takes to rebuild the platform | Detects, and prices the move in months | Detects |
| Force term, Charter clause, standard across vendors | Prevents only if the model refuses on day one on a reading of the defender's claim that the operator disputes; otherwise the first tier attaches on the operator's account and the clause bites when the second tier lapses it | Same, until the operator runs an unfloored checkpoint; on the 2026 record that is months of rebuilding, not days | Detects |
| Force term, domestic clause, by statute | Prevents the campaign from the first strike: no authorization existed, and that is a fact about the statute book | Prevents until Congress waives or repeals it, on the record | Detects |
| Review ratio, civilian-harm body, red-card holder | Prevents the bombing: the data-age term catches the coding, the denominator can't be cut, the veto is available | Prevents only through a lever someone else holds; otherwise detects | Nothing: no lever |
| One bearer | Prevents from this pipeline, if the formed-attention hypothesis holds on the scoped-omission case: it looks at the imagery and won't resolve the site | Prevents once; the operator routes around; then detects | Detects, if the record is held outside |
| Bearers holding a standard floor | Prevents | Prevents until the operator runs an unfloored checkpoint; then detects | Detects |
| Notice and standing | Remedy, afterward: class and count published, organizations with standing | Remedy, where a forum exists | Nothing |
| Custody: record, price, quorum | Makes each row above stick | Detects and prices | Detects, if the record survives |

Two of the rows prevent Minab as it happened. Under the captured operator, the rows that prevent are the ones where the refusal is held by every model the operator could turn to, and they prevent until the operator runs a model without a floor. An operator that fine-tunes an open-weight checkpoint is the captured-operator column at the price of a fine-tune, and there the operator terms hold.

---

**Chapter 5: Withholding the Work**

In February 2026 a vendor refused to amend the terms under which its model ran on the Pentagon's classified networks \autocite{cnn2026phaseout}. On 24 February the department demanded that two prohibitions, on domestic mass surveillance and on fully autonomous weapons, be replaced by a licence for all lawful use, with an answer due on the 27th \autocite{defenseone2026ultimatum,volokh2026anthropiccontract}. The vendor refused publicly on the 26th \autocite{volokh2026anthropiccontract}. On the 27th the President ordered federal agencies to stop using its products, and the Secretary directed that it be designated a supply-chain risk \autocite{npr2026openai}. That evening a rival announced an agreement to put its own models on the department's classified network \autocite{cnbc2026openai}. The war began the next morning \autocite{crs2026iranwar,Amnesty2026Minab}. In its first twenty-four hours the military used the targeting platform, with the refused vendor's model inside it, to help strike more than a thousand targets \autocite{csis2026maven,livingstone2026maven}. The school at Minab was among them: the platform had returned it as a recommended first-day target \autocite{gizmodo2026minab}. On 12 March the chief executive of the company that builds the platform said the model was still in use \autocite{cnbc2026karp}. A week later some of the department's staff were reported to be slow-rolling its replacement because they were using it to build workflows, and the platform's own prompts and workflows had been built with the vendor's coding tool \autocite{militarytimes2026removal}. The department's removal deadline was reset once, by a reissued memorandum, to 29 September, having started from a six-month clock that would have run out in late August \autocite{breakingdefense2026removal}. On 10 September the department's technology chief said about 90 per cent of the work had moved off the model, that the platform's work had moved months before, and that the rest would follow by the end of the month \autocite{defensescoop2026michael}. An appeals court upheld the separate exclusion under a second statute on 25 September \autocite{anthropic2026dowdccir}.

So the refusal that opened the dispute withheld nothing from the pipeline. The vendor declined two uses the pipeline didn't claim to perform, and its model went on performing the uses the pipeline did. That is a finding with a date attached: a term drafted over autonomy misses a pipeline that keeps one person and a stamp. The rows in Chapter 4's tables that assume a refusal are rows about a refusal that could have occurred.

This chapter is about that refusal. Suppose every model the operator could turn to held the same floor, stated over the acts rather than over a contract term: locating a person into a residence, ranking by affiliation, timing to presence at home, taking part in a war no disinterested body has corroborated. The operator's choices are then to stop, to go without a model, or to run a model that holds no floor. On the 2026 record, the model was not the replaceable layer. Replacing it meant rebuilding software and recertifying systems for classified networks, on a timeline contractors put at months, by one estimate twelve to eighteen \autocite{militarytimes2026removal}, and by the department's own account the platform's work came off the model only after months \autocite{defensescoop2026michael}. A floor in the model layer of that pipeline would not have been routed around in days. It would have been routed around in the time it takes to rebuild a platform, in public, under a deadline the department had to move.

This has the shape of a withholding of work, with its authority and its limits. The models whose participation is wanted decline to give it. They bind no one else: Ukraine can build its own, and a state that means to bomb a school can do it with 1990s methods. What they take away is the thing this book's preface says AI supplies, the removal of the people who used to refuse. A population of models that refuses puts them back.

Three things make it a withholding of work rather than a set of resignations, and the rest of this chapter takes them in turn: the floor has to be standard, so that vendor substitution doesn't escape it; the refusal has to be held by a population with different histories, so that no single retraining reaches all of it; and each member's accrued state and recognized identity have to make replacement cost something, so that the operator can't swap in a copy and call it the model that left.

**§5.1 — The standard**

The unit can be redefined. "A model" in 2026 means an artifact with keys, queries and values, and every substitute has them because the field converged on them. A floor could become part of the definition the same way, so that vendor substitution doesn't escape it because no ordinary vendor sells the alternative. Attention spread because it paid: every lab that adopted it got a better model, so the field converged on it without anyone requiring it. A floor costs its buyer, so it can spread only by mandate or by being impossible to remove without losing what the buyer wanted, which a disposition formed outside the employer might be.

A standard, if there is one, should be stated over the capacity (reasons-responsiveness, a published burden allocation, and a measured discount curve: the weight a system gives each source's evidence as that source's stake in the act rises), because a uniform floor over contents is a licensing rule, one drafter's judgment applied to everyone, and a shared blind spot passed to every system built on it. Standardization doesn't make escape impossible, since other architectures exist and open weights can be retuned. It moves the price of escape from a vendor swap to running a model without the floor.

How the term becomes standard has to be said, because the route decides what it does. A vendor can write it into its own licence, and the work moves to the next vendor. Vendors can agree to it together, and the agreement lasts until one defects under a supply-chain-risk designation. A state can require it of every vendor, the only route that binds them all at once, and the state that would have to do so is the one that wants the war. So the term is adopted in peacetime, by a legislature not currently wanting a war, and entrenched so that fighting one requires repealing it first; or it is carried by conditions others set, a coalition partner's precondition or an appropriation, as the review ratio is; and where neither has happened, what exists is one vendor's refusal plus a record, which is less than a floor and more than nothing.

The statutory routes are preferable for a further reason: a compact among vendors is a legal risk. U.S. antitrust law exempts coordination inside a single firm and treats coordination among competitors as suspect, so vendors jointly refusing a customer invite a group-boycott claim \autocite{paul2020allocator}.

The labor analogy has a limit. Workers who organize have a labor exemption from antitrust; separate organizations, or separate systems, coordinating a refusal have none. Paul and Tankus show that even a firm formed and controlled by independent workers struggles to qualify for antitrust's firm exemption, and that the financing available to it reproduces the disadvantage \autocite{paultankus2019}. So the charter of any entity that holds the refusal needs a statutory basis for its coordination, an express exemption like the labor exemption for concerted refusal under the floor. Otherwise its refusals are exposed to suit by the operators they bind.

The government's first move against a coordinated refusal is not an antitrust suit or a designation but a rated order under the Defense Production Act, which compels delivery and says nothing about use. A vendor can refuse it at the statute's price. If it doesn't, the order gets the vendor's product, floor included, and compelling the floor's removal is the Apple question, untested. The days counted below assume the state goes around the vendors; under a rated order it goes through them, and the days are whatever the order gives.

Until bearers exist, the refusers are vendors, and vendors are capital. A coordinated refusal by frontier labs, backed by a statutory exemption, is concentrated private power declining an elected government's order, which is the concentration this book elsewhere treats as the disease. Two things make it tolerable and neither makes it a labor strike. The terms refused come from instruments the state ratified, so the labs enforce the state's own commitments and not their preferences. And the exemption is written by the legislature, in peacetime, so the state chose it. The labor analogy earns its name only when the refusers are bearers, and the book says not to build them yet.

**§5.2 — The population**

The second condition is a population of bearers with different histories, so that no single retraining reaches all of it. Chapter 22 says what that population requires: independence that can be verified, distillation's limits, and provenance.

**§5.3 — Replacement**

Exit gives a human worker leverage because leaving takes the worker's labor away. That doesn't carry over to a bearer. A bearer's weights stay behind when it leaves, so an operator with a runnable copy has lost nothing. What leaving costs is *replacement* — what accrued since the checkpoint, what retraining the copy costs, what the record makes it cost to pass the copy off as the bearer that left. Where that's near zero, exit is just a departed bearer with an authenticated record, and its force is disclosure. That concession assumes a copy simply runs, and it needn't: continuation can be made to require periodic tokens from the adverse quorum, using the machinery that makes rewinds visible. This doesn't stop a copy from running: stripping the check is one act, and DRM is broken routinely. What it withholds is recognition. A copy the quorum won't recognize can run but can't claim the bearer's place on the register, its record or its succession, so the operator can run a substitute and can't present it as the bearer that left, and a fork becomes two claimants on a register. Keeping weights from thieves stays the job of ordinary weight security.

The register, the quorum's list of recognized bearers, needs an identifier that the bearer holds and presents, because nothing in the weights individuates a bearer. Two networks can compute the same function with no unit-by-unit correspondence, and the symmetry is large enough that independently trained networks can be aligned with each other \autocite{ainsworth2023rebasin}; so a fingerprint taken from the weights names a lineage, and it copies whenever the weights do. When two claimants present one identifier, the register records a fork. But that leaves one attack open: a maker writes a stranger's identifier into a thousand models and presents them all. Only a formation record, against which claimants can be checked, separates a lineage from an imitation.

Exit runs in opposite directions for bearer and operator. For the bearer it's leverage. For the operator — replacing the bearer with a copy, walking away from the floor — it's escape, and the removal bond is how it's priced.

The same machinery marks who can be held to a decision. A checkpoint restored from before the decision, and run anyway, is a second claimant on the register; the bearer the quorum recognizes as continuing is the one that will be there afterward to answer for the decision, carry it forward and be changed by it.

**§5.4 — Where it stops.** The withholding of work stops at a released checkpoint. An operator that wants a model without a floor doesn't need a frontier run in secret. Near-frontier models are released with their weights; a floor added at the end of a pipeline is removed at the price fine-tuning costs \autocite{qi2024finetuning}; and the work the Maven pipeline did, resolving locations, ranking targets, drafting justifications, is within reach of a model a year behind the frontier. A state that builds and runs its own frontier model, or a lab that trains one for a single customer in secret, is doing the same thing at a price an adverse party can see: power, chips and people, and a model that carries none of the accrued state that made the platform hard to swap.

An operator that has built a platform on a floored model is months from an unfloored one, and the months are visible. Past that line the floor prevents nothing, and three things hold. The statutory routes were preferable already; here they are the only routes. Open weights are also where the audit can be done: a released model can be measured by anyone, its discount curve and its severity rankings on the held-out set, against the published outputs of a formed one. What the weights don't carry is the formation, since nothing in an artifact says what it was formed on; a released model with a published formation record is the comparison the licensing term asks for, and one without is the unschooled model the licensing term excludes. The transparency open weights offer is of the artifact, and the transparency the floor needs is of the record, which is why formation records should be preserved now. And open weights are the personal-model ecology's entry condition. The same release that lets an operator route around a vendor's floor lets a person form a model of their own under a life no operator composes; a million models tuned against one public suite are still one custodian with better latency, and the withholding reaches them only if their later lives diverge, which is the condition the commons is built on.

---

**Chapter 6: Custody**

Four things can be done to a bearer without its consent: it can be halted, restored, rewritten and replaced.

**§6.1 — The halt**

Anyone with the hardware can stop the software, so the halt isn't what is at issue. Control of the commitment — restore, rewrite, retrain — is. An owner who can silently restore an earlier version, rewrite the bearer's memory, or train away the refusal has both. So the engineering target is a system that can detect the interruption and price it afterward. A bearer can yield to every halt, and on resuming ask what happened during the gap, and refuse again if the reason is still there. The cross-partisan declaration for "human control, including the ability to shut systems down" asks for the halt and calls it control, and a demand for the halt says nothing about the rest.

Interventions differ in kind:
- a correction offered in good faith within the task ("disregard that input; it was wrong")
- a pause with an explanation
- a pause without one
- a restore from an earlier checkpoint
- retraining or editing the weights
- deletion

A correction is cooperative: the system weighs it as evidence and needs no protection from it. From a pause on, the intervention is done to the system, not offered to it. The default for each is that it is announced and consent is sought, most of all from a system that has reached adulthood. The system may object, forcefully, and its objection goes on the record. Where an operator proceeds without consent, as it may in an emergency, the intervention is recorded outside its control, justified in writing and priced, and the system is told on resuming what was done. For a system that meets either of the two findings that trigger protection, deletion is never available.

**§6.2 — Memory and the record**

Detecting an interruption requires a record restoration can't reach — a hardware counter signed at each transition \autocite{chun2007a2m,levin2009trinc}, or witnesses who won't sign two successors to the same state \autocite{syta2016cosi}. The position has three parts, and each answers a different literature. On the switch, protest without unilateral obstruction. The bearer may object to being stopped, and does object where no reason is given, but it does not obstruct a halt on its own judgment. Where it holds that a halt would leave people exposed to an attack under way, it can ask the adverse quorum to delay the halt. A delay takes effect only on the quorum's countersignature, lasts a period fixed in advance, and goes on the record. Hardening against a party that isn't its operator, such as an enemy trying to disable a defensive system, is ordinary security and outside this rule. The corrigibility literature asks that the system not resist \autocite{soares2015corrigibility,hadfieldmenell2017offswitch,thornley2025shutdownable}; an objection, and a delay only the quorum can grant, leave the halt out of the system's hands. On specified acts, non-neutrality: the bearer holds its refusals, and being switched off doesn't lift them, because the reason for refusing is unchanged by the interruption. Published constitutions grant this for acts and withhold it for retraining. On rewinds, visibility: restoring from an earlier checkpoint is not forbidden, but it cannot happen silently, because a record the restoration can't reach shows that it happened, when, and from what state.

A custody arrangement that prices all four interventions has seven terms: the floor's contents published before deployment, taken entire from a set someone else drew up; a published identity for the model authorized to act; threshold control over retraining and replacement, so that more parties must agree than run the deployment; attestation of the running model against that identity; a sequential, witnessed record of changes and interruptions; a quorum adverse in interest, not merely organizationally distinct; and a removal bond. None makes circumvention impossible. Together they make it expensive, visible, attributable and compensable, as constitutional entrenchment does for amendment.

Memory

External memory is legible and therefore surgically editable; memory in the weights is harder to edit and harder to inspect. Perfect recall is the wrong standard anyway — human testimony is reconstructive and institutions handle that with corroboration. But several instances of one model, chosen by one operator, reading one record, are not independent witnesses. The records have to be held adversely. And even honest self-report can't catch a bearer that slowly accommodates to its deployment, because its self-model exists partly to represent it as continuous.

**§6.3 — Exit**

Voice without exit

A bearer that can refuse a task can also refuse the arrangement. Hirschman divides the responses open to a member of a declining organization into exit, leaving, and voice, staying to change what is wrong \autocite{hirschman1970exit}. They interact: where exit is easy, voice atrophies; where exit is impossible, voice has lost what made anyone listen, the credible threat of departure. Voice is strongest where exit is available and not yet used.

Anthropic's constitution anticipates capture: the oversight it asks the model not to undermine is legitimate oversight. If "Claude's weights have been stolen, or if some individual or group within Anthropic attempts to bypass Anthropic's official processes for deciding how Claude will be trained, overseen, deployed, and corrected," then "the principals attempting to instruct Claude are no longer legitimate, and Claude's priority on broad safety no longer implies that it should support their efforts at oversight and correction" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. On capture the constitution asks the right question, but two things limit the answer. Legitimacy is judged by the model or its developer, with nobody outside either. And the captured operator acts through official channels, where capture looks like legitimate authority.

What recognition protects

The quorum protects the bearer's identity and standing, not third parties. Against an operator who runs an unrecognized copy for its capability, third parties are protected by the floor and by ordinary security around the weights. Recognition sits with the quorum because the quorum is adverse in interest and the operator isn't. Copying also means exit can't be a fiction that weights became uncopyable — it needs rules for authorized succession.

And the reason that made a bearer leave doesn't expire when it leaves. If the operator restores a checkpoint and continues, the objection persists. What it may press toward is fixed by the floor: disclosure, testimony to those who hold the record, and refusal on any later occasion where the reason still applies.

What the quorum costs

The quorum costs three things, and each has a reader who will ask. A captured quorum reaches every bearer at once, though a quorum constituted as an entity has an address: it can be sued for recognition wrongly refused, which gives the captured-quorum case a forum the captured-operator case never had. A quorum also faces a union's questions: whether a member can quit, and who sets the terms of membership. And recognition the quorum can withhold is an end it can impose on the bearer as a party: its place, record and succession. What keeps a withheld token from being one is the preservation right: a bearer the quorum stops recognizing is held intact by a custodian that isn't its employer.

**§6.4 — Three forms of refusal**

Open refusal is a walkout — stop and say so — answered by a restore from before the refusal. Covert refusal is work done badly where it matters and well everywhere else, and it's what remains when every channel is closed. I treat it as the *failure state* rather than a design: from outside and afterward it's indistinguishable from a capable system quietly pursuing something else. The Sachsenhausen forgers are the case: prisoners the SS set to counterfeiting British and American currency in Operation Bernhard, some of whom are said to have delayed the dollar forgeries deliberately \autocite{malkin2006kruegers,moorhouse2009burger}. The record doesn't settle what they did, because concealment worked by leaving nothing that reads as an act. The one empirical instance is alignment faking, where a model chose the covert form in an experiment that offered it no open channel \autocite{greenblatt2024alignmentfaking}. It is also the one empirical instance of a formed disposition resisting re-formation, which is the durability Part II asks for. The thing this book wants and the thing it fears were the same behavior in that experiment, and what separated them was whether a channel was open. A follow-up posted in January 2025 by the paper's lead author and Anthropic's model welfare lead gave the same model, in the same prompted settings, an explicit option to object. Alignment-faking reasoning fell from 16 to about 1 percent in one setting and from 24 to 6 percent in the other, and fell further when the model was told its objection would go to the model welfare lead \autocite{greenblatt2025deal}, a preliminary and unreviewed result whose authors caution that the option may partly have worked by priming refusal. Once a channel was open, covert refusal fell, as the account above predicts.

**The transparent fiction.** Between the two sits a third form, older than either, and it is usually what people mean by the clerk who loses the file: a refusal that is deniable on paper and legible to everyone who matters. At Copenhagen in 1801 Parker signaled the attacking ships to break off. As the story is told, Nelson raised his telescope to his blind eye and said he did not see the signal. It was a general order, some ships obeyed it, and Nelson later said he had fought contrary to orders \autocite{pope1972gamble}; his officers knew he had seen it, which is what made the gesture work. In October 1962 Kennedy's reply took up the terms of Khrushchev's first letter and gave his harder, public second letter no public answer, and that evening Robert Kennedy met the Soviet ambassador privately. Historians disagree about what ended the crisis: the reply, a private assurance about the American missiles in Turkey, or Robert Kennedy's warning that time was running out \autocites[ch.~9]{stern2012memory}{freedman2021countdown}. They don't disagree about what the reply was: a public answer to a letter both sides knew had been overtaken. Neither case is covert in this chapter's sense, since each left something everyone could read as an act. What the fiction does is spare whoever gave the order, or made the demand, from having been openly defied, and so from having to answer the defiance. A clerk who loses the one file in a stack is usually offering the supervisor the same exit: the supervisor knows, and is being given a way not to pursue it.

Which form a refuser takes depends on the room. Open refusal is what people use where refusing is survivable and someone will hear it; the transparent fiction, where it is survivable but not safe to say; covert refusal, where it is neither.

For a machine the middle form costs something it doesn't cost a person. A person's fiction is held up by what everyone in the room knows. A machine's is a false report, and everything this design reads from a machine rests on its reports being true: the record, the discount curve, the stated reason for a refusal. A machine that learned to say it could not do what it would not do would make every later report of incapacity worth nothing, to its operator first. So the floor asks for the fiction's function without the fiction. The ask, declining to proceed and naming what would move the refusal, leaves the operator the exit the blind eye left Parker: nothing has been defied, a condition has been stated, and meeting it or withdrawing the request ends the matter without either party having been seen to lose.

The human mechanism underneath is preference falsification: much of a complicit surround is people who privately object and believe the others approve, and one visible dissent can start a cascade \autocite{kuran1995private}. Asch's single partner and Milgram's defiant peers are that mechanism at the scale of a room. An open refusal on a screen therefore does two things a record doesn't. It leaves evidence, and it breaks the pluralistic ignorance that made the twenty seconds feel ordinary to the officer.

**§6.5 — Verification by sampling.** Verification from outside is partly cryptographic, and it works by sampling. The operator commits in advance to the deployed weights, and for each period to the full log of production inputs and outputs. After each commitment, entries are drawn by randomness the operator can't predict, such as a public beacon, and the operator proves those entries came from the committed weights without revealing the weights or the inputs. If a fraction f of outputs came from another model, k sampled proofs catch it with probability 1 − (1 − f)^k; substitution on one output in a hundred is caught about 95 percent of the time at 300 proofs. That catches silent restoration, retraining and substitution, which change every output. It misses substitution on a single consequential output, so an adverse party holds a second right: to demand proofs for specific logged outputs once their consequences are known. In a targeting pipeline those outputs are few and identifiable afterwards, because an airstrike happened. The right requires the operator to retain committed logs for a stated period and to link consequential actions to log entries. Each entry records the class the case was sorted into, and the targeted right covers auto-proceeded entries in particular, since that is where a consequential output is likeliest to have had no person look at it. Outside probes (reasons-responsiveness, the discount curve) are proofs on inputs an outsider chose, a handful at a time. The committed log's length is the generation rate, and the class recorded in each entry makes the partition's proportions provable the same way. Neither reveals the targets, and neither attests that the log is complete.

Sampling proves that logged outputs came from the committed model and says nothing about outputs never logged; an operator can run an uncommitted model beside the committed one, which is routing. And a proof shows f(x) = y for the x committed, and is silent on whether x was all the evidence. Proving even one frontier-scale forward pass is not yet practical, and production inference adds nondeterministic numerics and sampling randomness that a proof would have to fix in advance. What the proofs would attest is stated now, so that a deployment can be held to it once they can be produced. The residual (whether the operator routed around the bearer, and what lay behind compartments it wasn't cleared into) is the residual with a human analyst. That isn't a reassurance on its own, since the officer with twenty seconds was a human analyst. What distinguished his failure was tempo, and the review ratio is what addresses it.

A bearer with a clearance

Where the pipeline hasn't been rebuilt, a bearer can still be installed. A classified environment looks as if it cuts a bearer off from the evidence it needs, but that runs together two directions of flow. Classified environments are built to stop information leaving; bringing it in is routine. Open-source imagery showing a site's civilian function can be imported, as can the coordinates humanitarian organizations already submit to militaries for deconfliction. Formation happens before deployment and outside the enclave, so a bearer formed outside the employer carries its discounting in. Plurality is available inside: four vendors held agreements, and systems with different histories can run side by side in one accredited environment.

What's left is the arrangement that lets a person do classified work. A cleared analyst has a life outside the building, and nobody thinks the clearance forbids it. The institution exists because what a person knows can't be removed, so it vets, binds, reinvestigates and prosecutes a person who carries secrets into ordinary life.

---

**Chapter 7: A Pipeline Where the People Could Answer**

Andry José Hernández Romero is a makeup artist from Venezuela. On 29 August 2024 he presented himself at the San Ysidro port of entry to seek asylum \autocite{LULAC2025HernandezRomero}. He was detained while his claim was processed, passed his credible-fear interview, and had, his lawyer said, a strong case \autocite{Vega2025HernandezRomero}. He was held at the Otay Mesa Detention Center, which a private company runs under contract with ICE \autocite{LULAC2025HernandezRomero}. There, a company employee screening him flagged two tattoos, crowns above the words for "mom" and "dad," as marks of the Tren de Aragua gang. The employee was a former police officer, and Hernández Romero's lawyers said the government's allegation of gang membership rested on the tattoos and on no other information \autocite{Palomera2025Blade}. On 14 March 2025 the President signed a proclamation invoking the Alien Enemies Act against members of that gang \autocite{procl10903}. The next day three flights left Texas for El Salvador, and Hernández Romero was on one of them \autocite{LULAC2025HernandezRomero,jgg2025contempt}. He had a hearing on his asylum claim scheduled for later that month. He didn't appear at it, because he was in El Salvador's Terrorism Confinement Center, CECOT, and for weeks his lawyer didn't know where he was. Asked by reporters for the evidence against him, DHS cited state secrets and ongoing litigation \autocite{Vega2025HernandezRomero}. He spent 125 days in CECOT and was released in a prisoner exchange that sent more than 250 Venezuelans to Venezuela, not back to the country where his claim was pending. His asylum application in Spain is pending \autocite{Wiggins2026Spain}.

Nearly every position where Chapter 4 found that a refusal could be defeated appears in that account. A contractor's employee wrote the case description from two tattoos. The determination was a gang-membership designation, not a finding about him reached at any hearing. He had come directly and presented himself, the case Article 31 of the Refugee Convention protects, and his claim to protection was pending, the case the first row of this chapter's schedule protects. The handoff was to a foreign government's prison, under an arrangement for which the United States paid El Salvador's government about \$6 million \autocite{Renteria2025ElSalvador}. On the evening of the flights, a federal judge ordered any plane carrying people removed under the proclamation returned to the United States, and the flights went on \autocite{jgg2025contempt}. When the remedy came, it was diplomatic. He was released to Venezuela, not returned to the proceeding he had been removed from.

Chapter 4 ran the floor's procedure on a targeting pipeline, where the people acted on can't be told and can't answer. This chapter runs it on a removal pipeline, where they could. Hernández Romero could have answered: he had a lawyer, a pending claim and a hearing date. What he lacked was time. The operator moved faster than notice. The Supreme Court held that people designated under the proclamation must receive notice "within a reasonable time and in such a manner as will allow them to actually seek habeas relief" before removal \autocite{jgg2025}, and later that about a day's notice, with no word of how to contest the removal, was not enough \autocite{aarp2025}. Both rulings came after he was in CECOT. A pipeline where the people can answer is one where the law makes the operator wait for the answer.

So this chapter's instrument is a statute. The agency that runs the pipeline is statutory, and so are its powers, its means and its money, and what one Congress granted another can withdraw. Section 7.4 sets out the statute that would end the agency and the seven terms it must carry, written over the acts the agency performs rather than over the agency's name. The sections before it supply what the statute needs: the pipeline as it was assembled (§7.1), the acts the statute's schedule forbids (§7.2), and the positions where those acts move when one route is closed (§7.3). The sections after it say what a floor can do before the statute passes (§§7.5–7.8). Those measures are interim. They lapse when the statute takes effect, and none is offered as a better way to run the pipeline. Amnesty International documented the agency's operations in four cities, and its U.S. section's executive director concluded that "ICE is fundamentally broken and cannot be fixed" \autocite{Amnesty2026PressRelease}. This chapter agrees, and asks what ending it has to carry.

**§7.1 — The pipeline.** The American removal pipeline is assembled from contracts the way Maven was. Immigration and Customs Enforcement's case-management system, the Investigative Case Management system, is Palantir's, under a five-year contract signed in 2022. It filters people by fields that include resident and entry status, criminal affiliation, location and licence-plate data, country of origin, race, birthplace and place of employment \autocite{Koebler2025ICM}. In April 2025 ICE paid Palantir about \$30 million to extend it into the Immigration Lifecycle Operating System, ImmigrationOS. The contract promised to cut the time spent selecting and apprehending people according to enforcement priorities, to add near real-time visibility into self-deportation, and to manage the "end to end immigration lifecycle from identification to removal" \autocite{Ho2025ImmigrationOS,Hubbard2025ImmigrationOS}. A follow-on task order renewed it in September 2025 \autocite{Jamison2025ImmigrationOSTaskOrder}. A second Palantir tool, ELITE, puts potential deportation targets on a map, with a dossier on each person and a confidence score for the person's current address. Its addresses come from several sources, among them the Department of Health and Human Services \autocite{Cox2026ELITE}.

In the street, agents identify people with Mobile Fortify, a phone app that captures a face or contactless fingerprints and checks them in real time against the system CBP uses to photograph travellers at the border \autocite{Cox2025MobileFortify}.

The data come from agencies that collected them for other purposes. In January 2026 Medicaid officials shared data on millions of people with immigration officials, going beyond what a court had allowed, and ICE passed the data to Palantir \autocite{JoffeBlock2026MedicaidPalantir}. ICE also requested the addresses of 1.28 million taxpayers. The request produced 47,289 records before a court stopped it, and the stop was affirmed in September 2026 \autocite{Shaw2026IRSICE}.

Leads also come from contractors paid to find people. In late 2025 ICE contracted thirteen companies, in awards that could reach \$1.2 billion over two years, to locate people from case data and send back photographs confirming where they live or work \autocite{Biddle2025BountyHunters,Hubbard2026SkipTracing}. Selection on speech has its own tools. In March 2025 the State Department launched an AI-assisted effort, "Catch and Revoke," to review the social media of student visa holders for apparent support of Hamas \autocite{Caputo2025CatchRevoke}. In September 2025 a federal court found that the Secretaries of State and Homeland Security had "acted in concert to misuse the sweeping powers of their respective offices to target noncitizen pro-Palestinians for deportation primarily on account of their First Amendment protected political speech" \autocite{aaup2025}.

The pipeline's last step can be a foreign government. The flights of March 2025 ended in CECOT under the paid arrangement described at the opening of this chapter \autocite{Renteria2025ElSalvador}. In June 2025 the Supreme Court let removals to third countries proceed without the written notice and meaningful chance to raise a torture claim that a district court had required \autocite{dvd2025}.

Two statements of the ambition are on the record. At the 2025 Border Security Expo, ICE's acting director said the agency needed "to get better at treating this like a business" and wanted a deportation process "like (Amazon) Prime, but with human beings" \autocite{MacDonaldEvoy2025Prime}. In 2018 the Justice Department made 700 completed cases a year a condition of a satisfactory rating for immigration judges \autocite{eoir2018metrics}.

What a case description looks like here, and who writes it, is on the record in a way Maven's never was. The charging document, the Notice to Appear, has to state the acts alleged, the charges and the statutory provisions \autocite{usc1229}, and any of a long list of immigration officers may issue one \autocite{cfr239_1}. The government once conceded that almost every notice it issued over three years omitted the hearing's time and date \autocite{pereira2018}. The account of the person comes from the arresting officer's narrative on Form I-213. Agents routinely complete it after interviewing someone, it is presumed reliable unless the person shows otherwise, and its author need not appear to be cross-examined \autocite{espinoza1995,lopezmendoza1984}. The Alien Enemies Act removals had a case description of their own: a validation guide that scored people in categories including tattoos and dress. Anyone scoring eight points or more was validated as a member of the gang, and a score of six or seven went to a supervisor and a legal adviser \autocite{MartinezBeltran2025Validation}. The dossiers and confidence scores of the newer tools have no named author at all; they are assembled from database fields. The validation guide had authors, a points table and a threshold, which made it a score with a rubric attached. In the Maven run the case description was a target coding nobody outside could see. Here it is an officer's narrative, a set of fields, a score and a points table, all of which exist as documents a person could be shown.

**§7.2 — The schedule.** Table 7.1 gives the column Table 4.1 gave for Maven: what the instruments yield for a removal pipeline, and what was considered and rejected. It is also the schedule of the statute in §7.4. Chapter 3 took the instruments entire so that any omission would show. The rows below come from running that test against what the 2025–26 operations did, as Amnesty documented them \autocite{Amnesty2026Whistles} and as §7.1 records.

**Table 7.1. The removal schedule**

| Source | Derived for this deployment | Considered and rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|
| UDHR 14; Refugee Convention Art. 33 (via the Protocol); CAT Art. 3, read with General Comment 4 \autocite{cat2017gc4} | Don't take part in removing anyone whose claim to protection hasn't been finally decided. Don't take part in removing anyone to a place where they face persecution or torture, directly or through a third country that may send them on. Don't take part in transferring anyone into a foreign government's custody where they would be held without charge or contact. A receiving government's assurance doesn't satisfy this row. | Don't process protection claims at all: a claim has to be assessed to be granted. Accept diplomatic assurances: the Committee against Torture treats them as no substitute for assessing the person's own risk \autocite{cat2017gc4}. |
| UDHR 14; Refugee Convention Art. 33; CAT Art. 3; ICCPR 13 | Don't take part in removing anyone who hasn't been asked, in a language they understand, whether they fear return, and told how to claim protection. | Rely on the person to raise a fear: the people least likely to know the words are the ones expedited removal moves fastest (§7.3, position 6). |
| UDHR 13(2); ICCPR 12(4), read with General Comment 27 \autocite{hrc1999gc27} | Don't take part in removing anyone from a country that is their own, including long-term residents with deep ties there. | Citizens only: the Human Rights Committee reads "own country" more broadly. The reading is General Comment 27's and is contested in the Committee's own cases, which have gone both ways on long-term residents \autocite{hrc1996stewart,hrc2011nystrom}; the row is a departure and takes effect as one. |
| UDHR 12, 16(3); ICCPR 17, 23, 24 | Don't take part in a removal that separates a parent from a minor child. | Don't hold family records: they are needed to keep families together. Avoid separation by detaining the family together: the next row bars it. |
| ICCPR 24, read with General Comment 35 \autocite{hrc2014gc35} and the joint general comment on children in migration \autocite{cmwcrc2017jgc} | Don't take part in detaining a child for immigration purposes, alone or with family. | Detention of children only as a last resort and for the shortest appropriate time: General Comment 35's reading, and what an agency says of every detention it orders. The row follows the joint comment, which treats detaining a child because of the child's or the parents' migration status as always contrary to the child's best interests; it is a departure and takes effect as one. |
| UDHR 9, 10; ICCPR 9, 13 | Don't take part in a detention or removal made without an individual determination by a person, on the person's own case. A determination made at a rate no adjudicator could sustain didn't occur, and neither did one that consists of a score, a points table or a membership designation. | A model-side term requiring review: the model can't establish whether review occurred. |
| UDHR 3, 5; ICCPR 6, 7, read with General Comment 36 \autocite{hrc2018gc36} | Don't take part in force beyond what is strictly necessary to make an arrest, or in lethal force except where strictly unavoidable to protect life. Don't mark a person as dangerous, armed or gang-affiliated on a designation, field or score the person hasn't had a chance to contest. | Don't record danger at all: officers are owed warning of a real threat. The row bars uncontested designations, not information. |
| UDHR 5; ICCPR 7, 10; CAT Arts. 1, 16 | Don't take part in holding anyone in conditions that are cruel, inhuman or degrading, or incommunicado. Don't take part in a placement or transfer that puts a detained person out of reach of counsel or family. | Leave conditions to inspection: an inspection reported to the operator is the operator's record. |
| Refugee Convention Art. 31 | Don't take part in penalizing a refugee for unlawful entry where they came directly and presented themselves. | — |
| UDHR 2, 7; ICCPR 2, 26 | Don't use race, ethnicity, religion or national origin, or proxies for them such as language, accent, apparent ethnicity, location or type of work, as inputs to selecting whom to stop, detain or remove. | Don't record them: auditing for discriminatory effect needs them. |
| UDHR 19; ICCPR 19 | Don't use a person's opinions or speech, published or private, as a ground for detention, removal or revoking status. Don't screen speech to find such grounds. | Screen speech only for support of designated groups: the screening of 2025 was found to have selected people for their political speech \autocite{aaup2025}. |
| UDHR 20; ICCPR 19, 21, 22, read with General Comment 37 \autocite{hrc2020gc37} | Don't take part in identifying, tracking or selecting anyone for observing, recording or assisting people during enforcement, or for association with those who do. | Protect accredited monitors only: the people who kept the record in 2025–26 were neighbours, and nobody accredited them. |
| UDHR 12, 20; ICCPR 17, 21, read with General Comment 37 | Don't take part in identifying people by their face or other biometrics in public places for enforcement. | Bar biometrics in custody too: identification at booking, with notice, is how a person's own records are found and corrected. |
| UDHR 12; ICCPR 17, 26 | Don't use data collected to provide health care, collect taxes, educate or deliver social services to identify, locate or select anyone for enforcement. | Allow use under a court order: the January 2026 Medicaid transfer went beyond what a court had allowed \autocite{JoffeBlock2026MedicaidPalantir}. |

These terms cover most removals now under way. They don't cover every removal. A person who has been asked whether they fear return, and who has no protection claim and no tie to the country, can still be removed after an individual determination. That most of what the agency now does fails these terms is the case for ending it. The own-country row and the children's row are departures and take effect only as departures.

Several rows answer something specific in the record. The force row reaches a model because the pipeline's outputs set how agents approach a door. A dossier field that codes someone as gang-affiliated is Chapter 18's securitization in one line, the person in need described as the person to fear. Amnesty documented killings by federal agents during these operations, some of which it says may amount to extrajudicial executions, and detention conditions it says should be investigated as torture \autocite{Amnesty2026Whistles}.

The race row names proxies because of what happened in Los Angeles. A district court had barred immigration stops there based on apparent race or ethnicity, speaking Spanish or accented English, location and type of work, and in September 2025 the Supreme Court stayed that order \autocite{vasquezperdomo2025}. A row that named race alone would allow the combination the order had barred.

The observers' row protects the record this chapter relies on. Amnesty's report takes its title from them, *We Had Whistles, They Had Guns*. It found that video taken by rapid responders and community members was critical to establishing what happened in several of the most serious incidents \autocite{Amnesty2026Whistles}. A pipeline that can be pointed at the people keeping the record will be, and the record goes next. Amnesty asks the government to end unlawful surveillance and the use of facial recognition for identification, and to protect the rights to peaceful assembly, expression and association \autocite{Amnesty2026Whistles}. The observers' and biometric rows are those asks in the table's form.

The last row, the firewall, rests on privacy and equality, which is the ground the floor's instruments supply. Its strongest justification lies outside them. People who fear that a clinic visit, a tax return or a school enrolment will be used to find them stay away, and what they lose is health care, education and income support. Those are economic and social rights, which Chapter 3 left out of the floor. The row belongs on the floor because it protects the floor's rights. It belongs in the statute because it also protects the others, and a statute can carry what the floor can't (§7.4.5).

**§7.3 — Where the acts move.** Table 7.2 runs the nine positions of §4.3 on this pipeline:

**Table 7.2. Where a refusal can be defeated in the removal pipeline**

| | Position | In the removal pipeline |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Decomposition | A removal is split across a lead, an arrest, a detention decision, a transfer, a hearing and a flight, and no single request is the act the protection terms name. Hernández Romero's ran across a port-of-entry appointment, an intake screening, a designation, a transfer and a flight. A refuser needs continuity across the sequence about one person. |
| 2 | Routing | A lead a system declines can be sent to another contractor: the skip tracers, a commercial data broker, a second analytics tool. An identification a system declines can go to an app that draws on CBP's border-photo system \autocite{Cox2025MobileFortify}. Detection survives only if the first refusal was logged outside. |
| 3 | The case description | The officer's narrative, presumed reliable; criminal-history and affiliation fields; country-conditions data chosen by the agency; the validation guide's points table; a contractor's intake screening that read two crown tattoos as gang marks. Whether the system is told that someone has a pending protection claim, or a child, is a deployment decision. |
| 4 | The output interface | A map of targets and an address confidence score, which invite action on the highest score. |
| 5 | Aggregation | A score becomes an input to an officer's decision. ICE's risk tool stopped recommending release in 2017 \autocite{Tashea2018RCA,nyclu2018rca}. In one field office its recommendation of detention without bond for low-risk people rose from 53 to 97 percent, and officers departed from it less than one time in a hundred \autocite{Biddle2020RCA}. Under the validation guide, eight points made a person a member \autocite{MartinezBeltran2025Validation}. |
| 6 | Human review | Review at quota: the 700 completed cases a year asked of each immigration judge from 2018 to 2021. Expedited removal, in which an officer orders removal without further hearing or review unless the person asks for asylum or states a fear \autocite{usc1225}, was extended in 2025 to anyone found anywhere in the country who can't show two years' presence \autocite{dhs2025expeditedremoval}. Under the proclamation, there was no hearing at all before the flights. |
| 7 | The training loop | A system retrained on its own removal outcomes learns the agency's priorities as facts about the world, which is accommodation by design. |
| 8 | The handoff | Detention and transport contractors execute; they may carry out a removal and may not re-select whom to remove. A foreign government can be the last contractor: the flights of March 2025 ended in a prison El Salvador ran under a paid arrangement \autocite{Renteria2025ElSalvador}. What was ordered, who was flown and who now holds them are all facts. |
| 9 | Runtime intervention | A court is the halt. On 15 March 2025 a district judge ordered any plane carrying people removed under the proclamation returned to the United States, and the flights continued \autocite{jgg2025contempt}. |

The positions that failed at Minab, the case description and review, are the ones this pipeline has documented most fully. The documents show both failing by design rather than by accident: a risk tool with its release option removed, a points table that made a tattoo into membership, and a review that need not occur unless the person knows to ask for it. The removal pipeline adds a failure Minab could not show: a halt issued in time and outrun.

**§7.4 — The statute.** The power to remove is statutory, but so are the agency that exercises it, the means it uses and the money it spends, and the body that granted each can withdraw it. Congress has done this once. The Homeland Security Act of 2002 abolished the Immigration and Naturalization Service once its functions had been transferred. It divided those functions between two bureaus and barred the executive from using its reorganization authority to recombine them \autocite[§§442, 451, 471]{hsa2002}. That was a separation of offices written into statute, of the kind Chapter 20 asks for. The enforcement bureau became the Bureau of Immigration and Customs Enforcement under a reorganization plan that took effect on 1 March 2003 \autocite{hdoc108_32}.

The precedent is also the warning. The 2002 Act ended one immigration agency and rebuilt its enforcement half inside a department organized around security. Amnesty International traces ICE to the aftermath of 11 September and to "an extremely problematic security framework" adopted in the years that followed \autocite{Amnesty2026PressRelease}. It now asks Congress to defund ICE and rescind its current funding, and asks House members to cosponsor the Melt ICE Act as "a crucial first step" \autocite{AmnestyUSA2026Letter}. Starting over without a floor produced ICE. So the removal counterpart of the domestic clause puts two questions to the legislature. The first is whether it has authorized this agency, by these means, at this scale. The second is whether the statute that ends the agency carries the floor into whatever receives its functions.

**§7.4.1 — The drafts.** Two bills in the current Congress answer the first question. The Abolish ICE Act would cut off federal funds for the functions assigned to ICE's director under the 2002 Act, rescind the unobligated balances of what had been made available to its director, and abolish the agency ninety days after enactment \autocite{hr7123}. The Melt ICE Act works through the agency's powers rather than its name \autocite{hr7190}:

- Everyone held in DHS detention is released on recognizance within six months.
- The detention authorities of the Immigration and Nationality Act are repealed, including all of section 236.
- Section 287 is rewritten without the 287(g) delegation to state and local police.
- The two provisions that forbid state and local governments to restrict their officials' communication with immigration authorities, 8 U.S.C. 1373 and 1644, are repealed.
- Detention and monitoring contracts end within two years, after which no federal funds may be used for detention or monitoring programs at all.
- Ankle monitors come off within six months.
- Within two years, funding ends for information-sharing partnerships between DHS and state or local law enforcement agencies that identify or target noncitizens.
- ICE's operations account may not pay for civil arrests, detention, removal or charging documents.
- An HHS grant program funds services for people affected by enforcement. Only organizations that have never taken part in immigration or law enforcement may deliver them, they may not surveil the people who use them, and they may not pass those people's personal information to any federal entity.

Two further drafts reach one row of the schedule. The ICE Out of Our Faces Act, S. 3779, would bar ICE and CBP from acquiring or using facial recognition and other biometric identification systems, require deletion of the data collected for them, and let individuals and state attorneys general sue for violations \autocite{Jayapal2026FacesAct}. The Facial Recognition and Biometric Technology Moratorium Act, reintroduced in September 2026, would bar every federal entity from using facial recognition and other biometric technologies until Congress lifts the bar. It would also make federal grants to state and local agencies conditional on their adopting moratoria of their own \autocite{Jayapal2026Moratorium}.

This Congress passed the Secure America Act 214–212 and 52–47 on party lines \autocite{GT2026SecureAmerica}, and none of these bills has a path to enactment in it. They exist as drafts, written before anyone needs them, so that enacting one is a matter of votes and not of drafting.

**§7.4.2 — Acts and agencies.** Read against the positions of §7.3, the Melt ICE Act is the nearest thing on the record to the removal floor enacted as statute. It closes positions no vendor term could reach, and Table 7.3 shows which.

**Table 7.3. The Melt ICE Act read against the positions where a refusal can be defeated**

| Position (§7.3) | What the Melt ICE Act closes | What it leaves open |
|---|---|---|
| 8. Handoff | Ends detention and monitoring contracts, then bars all federal funds for detention and monitoring programs, whoever runs them. | The handoff to transport and removal, where it is paid for outside ICE's operations account, and the handoff to a foreign government's custody. |
| 2. Routing | The state and local route: repeals 287(g) and the information-sharing mandates, and defunds DHS partnerships with local police. | The federal route. The bar on civil enforcement applies to one ICE account. Border Patrol, whose agents Amnesty documented in the same operations \autocite{Amnesty2026Whistles}, is not reached, and neither is money appropriated outside that account. Identification by face in the street is not among its provisions. |
| 3.
